</div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right;"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>William Lyon Mackenzie King</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_Merit" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of the Order of Merit">OM</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_St_Michael_and_St_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of St Michael and St George">CMG</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a></span> (December 17, 1874 – July 22, 1950) was a <a href="/wiki/Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian">Canadian</a> statesman and <a href="/wiki/Politician" title="Politician">politician</a> who was the tenth <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> for three non-consecutive terms from 1921 to 1926, 1926 to 1930, and 1935 to 1948. A <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a>, he was the dominant politician in Canada from the early 1920s to the late 1940s.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> King is best known for his leadership of Canada throughout the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression" title="Great Depression">Great Depression</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a>. He played a major role in laying the foundations of the Canadian <a href="/wiki/Welfare_state" title="Welfare state">welfare state</a> and established Canada's international reputation as a <a href="/wiki/Middle_power" title="Middle power">middle power</a> fully committed to world order.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> With a total of 21 years and 154 days in office, he remains the <a href="/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada_by_time_in_office" title="List of prime ministers of Canada by time in office">longest-serving prime minister in Canadian history</a>.
</p><p>King studied law and political economy in the 1890s and became concerned with issues of social welfare. He later obtained a PhD – the only Canadian prime minister to have done so. In 1900, he became deputy minister of the Canadian government's new Department of Labour. He entered the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="House of Commons (Canada)">House of Commons</a> in <a href="/wiki/1908_Canadian_federal_election" title="1908 Canadian federal election">1908</a> before becoming the federal <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Labour_(Canada)" title="Minister of Labour (Canada)">minister of labour</a> in 1909 under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>. After losing his seat in the <a href="/wiki/1911_Canadian_federal_election" title="1911 Canadian federal election">1911 federal election</a>, King worked for the <a href="/wiki/Rockefeller_Foundation" title="Rockefeller Foundation">Rockefeller Foundation</a> before briefly working as an industrial consultant. Following the death of Laurier in 1919, King <a href="/wiki/1919_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1919 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">acceded</a> to the leadership of the Liberal Party. Taking the helm of a party torn apart by the <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1917" title="Conscription Crisis of 1917">Conscription Crisis of 1917</a>, he unified both the pro-<a href="/wiki/Conscription" title="Conscription">conscription</a> and anti-conscription factions of the party, leading it to victory in the <a href="/wiki/1921_Canadian_federal_election" title="1921 Canadian federal election">1921 federal election</a>.
</p><p>King established a post-war agenda that lowered wartime taxes, moderately reduced tariffs, and developed the national capital, <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>. He strengthened Canadian autonomy by refusing to support Britain in the <a href="/wiki/Chanak_Crisis" title="Chanak Crisis">Chanak Crisis</a> without <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada" title="Parliament of Canada">Parliament</a>'s consent and negotiating the <a href="/wiki/Halibut_Treaty" title="Halibut Treaty">Halibut Treaty</a> with the United States without British interference. In the <a href="/wiki/1925_Canadian_federal_election" title="1925 Canadian federal election">1925 election</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(historical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservative Party of Canada (historical)">Conservatives</a> won a plurality of seats, but the Liberals negotiated support from the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Party of Canada">Progressive Party</a> and stayed in office as a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a>. In 1926, facing a Commons vote that could force his government to resign, King asked <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a> <a href="/wiki/Lord_Byng" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Byng">Lord Byng</a> to <a href="/wiki/Dissolve_parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Dissolve parliament">dissolve parliament</a> and call an election. Byng refused and instead invited the Conservatives to form government, who briefly held office but lost a <a href="/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence" title="Motion of no confidence">motion of no confidence</a>. This sequence of events triggered a major <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_crisis" title="Constitutional crisis">constitutional crisis</a>, the <a href="/wiki/King%E2%80%93Byng_affair" title="King–Byng affair">King–Byng affair</a>. King and the Liberals decisively won <a href="/wiki/1926_Canadian_federal_election" title="1926 Canadian federal election">the resulting election</a>. After, King sought to make Canada's foreign policy more independent by expanding the <a href="/wiki/Global_Affairs_Canada" title="Global Affairs Canada">Department of External Affairs</a> while recruiting more Canadian diplomats. His government also introduced <a href="/wiki/Old_Age_Security" title="Old Age Security">old-age pensions</a> based on need and removed taxes on cables, telegrams, and railway and steamship tickets. King's slow reaction to the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression_in_Canada" title="Great Depression in Canada">Great Depression</a> led to <a href="/wiki/1930_Canadian_federal_election" title="1930 Canadian federal election">a defeat at the polls in 1930</a>.
</p><p>The Conservative government's response to the depression was heavily unpopular, and King returned to power in a <a href="/wiki/Landslide_victory" title="Landslide victory">landslide victory</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1935_Canadian_federal_election" title="1935 Canadian federal election">1935 election</a>. Soon after, the economy was on an upswing. King negotiated the 1935 <a href="/wiki/Reciprocity_(Canadian_politics)" title="Reciprocity (Canadian politics)">Reciprocal</a> Trade Agreement with the United States, passed the 1938 <i><a href="/wiki/National_Housing_Act_(Canada)" title="National Housing Act (Canada)">National Housing Act</a></i> to improve housing affordability, introduced <a href="/wiki/Unemployment_benefits" title="Unemployment benefits">unemployment insurance</a> in 1940, and in 1944, introduced <a href="/wiki/Family_Allowance_Act" title="Family Allowance Act">family allowances</a> – Canada's first universal <a href="/wiki/Welfare_program" class="mw-redirect" title="Welfare program">welfare program</a>. The government also established <a href="/wiki/Trans-Canada_Air_Lines" title="Trans-Canada Air Lines">Trans-Canada Air Lines</a> (the precursor to <a href="/wiki/Air_Canada" title="Air Canada">Air Canada</a>) and the <a href="/wiki/National_Film_Board" class="mw-redirect" title="National Film Board">National Film Board</a>. Days after the Second World War <a href="/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland" title="Invasion of Poland">broke out</a>, Canadian troops were <a href="/wiki/Canada_in_World_War_II" title="Canada in World War II">deployed</a>. The Liberals' overwhelming triumph in the <a href="/wiki/1940_Canadian_federal_election" title="1940 Canadian federal election">1940 election</a> allowed King to continue leading Canada through the war. He mobilized Canadian money, supplies, and volunteers to support Britain while boosting the economy and maintaining morale on the home front. To satisfy <a href="/wiki/French_Canadians" title="French Canadians">French Canadians</a>, King delayed introducing overseas conscription until late 1944. <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II" title="Allies of World War II">The Allies</a>' <a href="/wiki/End_of_World_War_II_in_Europe" title="End of World War II in Europe">victory in 1945</a> allowed King to call <a href="/wiki/1945_Canadian_federal_election" title="1945 Canadian federal election">a post-war election</a>, in which the Liberals lost their <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a>. In his final years in office, King and his government partnered Canada with other <a href="/wiki/Western_nations" class="mw-redirect" title="Western nations">Western nations</a> to take part in the deepening <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a>, <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Citizenship_Act_1946" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Citizenship Act 1946">introduced</a> <a href="/wiki/Canadian_nationality_law" title="Canadian nationality law">Canadian citizenship</a>, and successfully negotiated <a href="/wiki/Dominion_of_Newfoundland" title="Dominion of Newfoundland">Newfoundland</a>'s <a href="/wiki/1948_Newfoundland_referendums" title="1948 Newfoundland referendums">entry</a> into <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Confederation" title="Canadian Confederation">Confederation</a>. A modernizing <a href="/wiki/Technocrat" class="mw-redirect" title="Technocrat">technocrat</a>, he wanted his Liberal Party to represent <a href="/wiki/Liberal_corporatism" title="Liberal corporatism">liberal corporatism</a> to create social harmony.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After leading his party for 29 years, and leading the country for <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1154941027">.mw-parser-output .frac{white-space:nowrap}.mw-parser-output .frac .num,.mw-parser-output .frac .den{font-size:80%;line-height:0;vertical-align:super}.mw-parser-output .frac .den{vertical-align:sub}.mw-parser-output .sr-only{border:0;clip:rect(0,0,0,0);clip-path:polygon(0px 0px,0px 0px,0px 0px);height:1px;margin:-1px;overflow:hidden;padding:0;position:absolute;width:1px}</style><span class="frac">21<span class="sr-only">+</span><span class="num">1</span>&#8260;<span class="den">2</span></span> years, King retired from politics in late 1948. He died of pneumonia in mid-1950. King's personality was complex; biographers agree on the personal characteristics that made him distinctive. He lacked the charisma of such contemporaries as <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle" title="Charles de Gaulle">Charles de Gaulle</a>. Cold and tactless in human relations, he lacked oratorical skill and his personality did not resonate with the electorate.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> He had many political allies but very few close personal friends. He kept secret his beliefs in <a href="/wiki/Spiritualism_(movement)" title="Spiritualism (movement)">spiritualism</a> and use of <a href="/wiki/Mediumship" title="Mediumship">mediums</a> to stay in contact with departed associates and particularly with his mother, and allowed his intense spirituality to distort his understanding of <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a> throughout the late 1930s.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Historian <a href="/wiki/Jack_Granatstein" class="mw-redirect" title="Jack Granatstein">Jack Granatstein</a> notes, "the scholars expressed little admiration for King the man but offered unbounded admiration for his political skills and attention to Canadian unity."<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> King is <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Canada">ranked</a> among the top three of Canadian prime ministers.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life_.281874.E2.80.931891.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_(1874–1891)">Early life (1874–1891)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life (1874–1891)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>King was born in a frame house rented by his parents at 43 Benton Street in Berlin (<a href="/wiki/Berlin_to_Kitchener_name_change" class="mw-redirect" title="Berlin to Kitchener name change">now</a> <a href="/wiki/Kitchener,_Ontario" title="Kitchener, Ontario">Kitchener</a>), Ontario to John King and Isabel Grace Mackenzie.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> His maternal grandfather was <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie" title="William Lyon Mackenzie">William Lyon Mackenzie</a>, first mayor of <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a> and leader of the <a href="/wiki/Upper_Canada_Rebellion" title="Upper Canada Rebellion">Upper Canada Rebellion</a> in 1837. His father was a lawyer and later a lecturer at <a href="/wiki/Osgoode_Hall_Law_School" title="Osgoode Hall Law School">Osgoode Hall Law School</a>. King had three siblings: older sister Isabel "Bella" Christina Grace (1873–1915), younger sister Janet "Jennie" Lindsey (1876–1962) and younger brother Dougall Macdougall "Max" (1878–1922).<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>  Within his family, he was known as Willie; during his university years, he adopted W. L. Mackenzie King as his signature and began using Mackenzie as his preferred name with those outside the family.
</p><p>King's father was a lawyer with a struggling practice in a small city, and never enjoyed financial security. His parents lived a life of shabby gentility, employing servants and tutors they could scarcely afford, although their financial situation improved somewhat following a move to Toronto around 1890, where King lived with them for several years in a duplex on Beverley Street while studying at the University of Toronto.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King became a lifelong practising <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian_Church_in_Canada" title="Presbyterian Church in Canada">Presbyterian</a> with a dedication to social reform based on his Christian duty.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> He never favoured <a href="/wiki/Socialism" title="Socialism">socialism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="University_.281891.E2.80.931900.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="University_(1891–1900)">University (1891–1900)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: University (1891–1900)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:King1899.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/King1899.jpg/170px-King1899.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="246" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/King1899.jpg/255px-King1899.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/ed/King1899.jpg 2x" data-file-width="332" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>King in 1899</figcaption></figure>
<p>King enrolled at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto</a> in 1891.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He obtained a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts" title="Bachelor of Arts">BA</a> degree in 1895, an <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Laws" title="Bachelor of Laws">LLB</a> degree in 1896, and an <a href="/wiki/Master_of_Arts" title="Master of Arts">MA</a> in 1897, all from the university.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> While studying in Toronto he met a wide circle of friends, many of whom became prominent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson195837–38_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson195837–38-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> He was an early member and officer of the <a href="/wiki/Kappa_Alpha_Society" title="Kappa Alpha Society">Kappa Alpha Society</a>, which included a number of these individuals (two future Ontario Supreme Court Justices and the future chairman of the university itself). It encouraged debate on political ideas. He also met <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Meighen" title="Arthur Meighen">Arthur Meighen</a>, a future political rival; the two men did not get on especially well from the start.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>King was especially concerned with issues of social welfare and was influenced by the <a href="/wiki/Settlement_movement" title="Settlement movement">settlement house movement</a> pioneered by <a href="/wiki/Toynbee_Hall" title="Toynbee Hall">Toynbee Hall</a> in London, England. He played a central role in fomenting a students' strike at the university in 1895. He was in close touch, behind the scenes, with Vice-Chancellor <a href="/wiki/William_Mulock" title="William Mulock">William Mulock</a>, for whom the strike provided a chance to embarrass his rivals Chancellor <a href="/wiki/Edward_Blake" title="Edward Blake">Edward Blake</a> and President <a href="/wiki/James_Loudon" title="James Loudon">James Loudon</a>. King failed to gain his immediate objective, a teaching position at the university but earned political credit with Mulock, the man who would invite him to <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a> and make him a <a href="/wiki/Deputy_minister" title="Deputy minister">deputy minister</a> only five years later.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> While studying at the University of Toronto, King also contributed to the campus newspaper, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Varsity_(newspaper)" title="The Varsity (newspaper)">The Varsity</a></i>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> King subsequently wrote for <i><a href="/wiki/The_Globe_(Toronto_newspaper)" title="The Globe (Toronto newspaper)">The Globe</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Mail_and_Empire" title="The Mail and Empire">The Mail and Empire</a></i>, and the <i>Toronto News</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Hardy-1948_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hardy-1948-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Fellow journalist <a href="/wiki/W._A._Hewitt" title="W. A. Hewitt">W. A. Hewitt</a> recalled that, the city editor of the <i>Toronto News</i> left him in charge one afternoon with instructions to fire King if he showed up. When Hewitt sat at the editor's desk, King showed up a few minutes later and resigned before Hewitt could tell him he was fired.<sup id="cite_ref-Hardy-1948_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hardy-1948-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After studying at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Chicago" title="University of Chicago">University of Chicago</a> and working with <a href="/wiki/Jane_Addams" title="Jane Addams">Jane Addams</a> at her settlement house, <a href="/wiki/Hull_House" title="Hull House">Hull House</a>, King proceeded to <a href="/wiki/Harvard_University" title="Harvard University">Harvard University</a>. He earned an MA in political economy from Harvard in 1898. In 1909, Harvard granted him a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Philosophy" title="Doctor of Philosophy">PhD</a> degree for a dissertation titled "Publicity and Public Opinion as Factors in the Solution of Industrial Problems in Canada."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958198–199_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958198–199-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He is the only Canadian Prime Minister to have earned a PhD.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_career.2C_civil_servant_.281900.E2.80.931908.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career,_civil_servant_(1900–1908)">Early career, civil servant (1900–1908)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Early career, civil servant (1900–1908)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingUniform1910.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/KingUniform1910.jpg/170px-KingUniform1910.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="221" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/KingUniform1910.jpg/255px-KingUniform1910.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/KingUniform1910.jpg/340px-KingUniform1910.jpg 2x" data-file-width="447" data-file-height="580" /></a><figcaption>Wearing court uniform as minister of labour in 1910</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1900, King became editor of the federal government-owned <i>Labour Gazette</i>, a publication that explored complex labour issues.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Later that year, he was appointed as deputy minister of the Canadian government's new Department of Labour, and became active in policy domains from Japanese immigration to railways, notably the <i>Industrial Disputes Investigations Act</i> (1907) which sought to avert labour strikes by prior conciliation.<sup id="cite_ref-thecanadianencyclopedia.com_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thecanadianencyclopedia.com-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1217611005"><div class="side-box side-box-right plainlinks sistersitebox"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409">
<div class="side-box-flex">
<div class="side-box-image"><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/38px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png" decoding="async" width="38" height="40" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/57px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/76px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="410" data-file-height="430" /></span></span></div>
<div class="side-box-text plainlist"><a href="/wiki/Wikisource" title="Wikisource">Wikisource</a> has original text related to this article:
<div style="margin-left: 10px;"><b><a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Secret_of_Heroism" class="extiw" title="wikisource:The Secret of Heroism">The Secret of Heroism</a></b></div></div></div>
</div>
<p>In 1901, King's roommate and best friend, <a href="/wiki/Henry_Albert_Harper" title="Henry Albert Harper">Henry Albert Harper</a>, died heroically during a skating party when a young woman fell through the ice of the partly frozen <a href="/wiki/Ottawa_River" title="Ottawa River">Ottawa River</a>. Harper dove into the water to try to save her, and perished in the attempt. King led the effort to raise a memorial to Harper, which resulted in the erection of the <a href="/wiki/Sir_Galahad" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Galahad">Sir Galahad</a> statue on <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Hill" title="Parliament Hill">Parliament Hill</a> in 1905. In 1906, King published a memoir of Harper, entitled <i>The Secret of Heroism</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958129–131_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958129–131-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While deputy minister of labour, King was appointed to investigate the causes of and claims for compensation resulting from the 1907 <a href="/wiki/Anti-Oriental_riots_(Vancouver)" title="Anti-Oriental riots (Vancouver)">anti-Oriental riots</a> in <a href="/wiki/Chinatown,_Vancouver" title="Chinatown, Vancouver">Vancouver's Chinatown</a> and <a href="/wiki/Japantown" title="Japantown">Japantown</a>. One of the claims for damages came from Chinese <a href="/wiki/Opium" title="Opium">opium</a> dealers, which led King to investigate <a href="/wiki/Narcotics" class="mw-redirect" title="Narcotics">narcotics</a> use in <a href="/wiki/Vancouver" title="Vancouver">Vancouver</a>, British Columbia. Following the investigation King reported that white women were also opium users, not just Chinese men, and the federal government used the report to justify the first legislation outlawing narcotics in Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_political_career.2C_minister_of_labour_.281908.E2.80.931911.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career,_minister_of_labour_(1908–1911)">Early political career, minister of labour (1908–1911)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Early political career, minister of labour (1908–1911)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>King was first elected to <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada" title="Parliament of Canada">Parliament</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1908_Canadian_federal_election" title="1908 Canadian federal election">1908 federal election</a>, representing <a href="/wiki/Waterloo_North" class="mw-redirect" title="Waterloo North">Waterloo North</a>. In 1909, King was appointed as the first-ever <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Labour_(Canada)" title="Minister of Labour (Canada)">minister of labour</a> by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King's term as minister of labour was marked by two significant achievements. He led the passage of the <i><a href="/wiki/Industrial_Disputes_Investigation_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Industrial Disputes Investigation Act">Industrial Disputes Investigation Act</a></i> and the <i><a href="/wiki/Combines_Investigation_Act" title="Combines Investigation Act">Combines Investigation Act</a></i>, which he had shaped during his civil and parliamentary service. The legislation significantly improved the financial situation for millions of Canadian workers.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>  In 1910 Mackenzie King introduced a bill aimed at establishing an 8-hour day on public works but it was killed in the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> He lost his seat in the <a href="/wiki/1911_Canadian_federal_election" title="1911 Canadian federal election">1911 general election</a>, which saw the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(historic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservative Party of Canada (historic)">Conservatives</a> defeat the Liberals and form government.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Out_of_politics_.281911.E2.80.931919.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Out_of_politics_(1911–1919)">Out of politics (1911–1919)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Out of politics (1911–1919)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Industrial_consultant">Industrial consultant</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Industrial consultant"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After his defeat, King went on the lecture circuit on behalf of the Liberal Party. In June 1914 <a href="/wiki/John_D._Rockefeller_Jr." title="John D. Rockefeller Jr.">John D. Rockefeller Jr.</a> hired him at the <a href="/wiki/Rockefeller_Foundation" title="Rockefeller Foundation">Rockefeller Foundation</a> in New York City, to head its new Department of Industrial Research. It paid $12,000 per year, compared to the meagre $2,500 per year the Liberal Party was paying.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958227–231_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958227–231-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> He worked for the Foundation until 1918, forming a close working association and friendship with Rockefeller, advising him through the turbulent period of the 1913–1914 Strike and <a href="/wiki/Ludlow_Massacre" title="Ludlow Massacre">Ludlow Massacre</a>–in what is known as the <a href="/wiki/Colorado_Coalfield_War" title="Colorado Coalfield War">Colorado Coalfield War</a>–at a family-owned coal company in <a href="/wiki/Colorado" title="Colorado">Colorado</a>, which subsequently set the stage for a new era in labour management in America.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> King became one of the earliest expert practitioners in the emerging field of <a href="/wiki/Industrial_relations" title="Industrial relations">industrial relations</a>.
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingLaurier1912.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/KingLaurier1912.jpg/150px-KingLaurier1912.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/KingLaurier1912.jpg/225px-KingLaurier1912.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/KingLaurier1912.jpg/300px-KingLaurier1912.jpg 2x" data-file-width="319" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>King standing behind former Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>, 1912</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingWriting.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/KingWriting.jpg/220px-KingWriting.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="130" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/KingWriting.jpg/330px-KingWriting.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/KingWriting.jpg/440px-KingWriting.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="378" /></a><figcaption>King, while writing <i>Industry and Humanity</i>, 1917</figcaption></figure>
<p>King was not a pacifist, but he showed little enthusiasm for the <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">Great War</a>; he faced criticism for not serving in Canada's military and instead working for the Rockefellers. But he was nearly 40 years old when the war began, and was not in good physical condition. He never gave up his Ottawa home, and travelled to the United States on an as-needed basis, performing service to the war effort by helping to keep war-related industries running smoothly.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958ch._10_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958ch._10-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1918, King, assisted by his friend F. A. McGregor, published <i>Industry and Humanity: A Study in the Principles Underlying Industrial Reconstruction</i>, a dense, abstract book he wrote in response to the <a href="/wiki/Ludlow_massacre" class="mw-redirect" title="Ludlow massacre">Ludlow massacre</a>. It went over the heads of most readers, but revealed the practical idealism behind King's political thinking. He argued that capital and labour were natural allies, not foes, and that the community at large (represented by the government) should be the third and decisive party in industrial disputes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958248–251_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958248–251-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> He expressed derision for syndicates and trades unions, chastising them for aiming at the "destruction by force of existing organization, and the transfer of industrial capital from the present possessors" to themselves.<sup id="cite_ref-Industry_and_Humanity_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Industry_and_Humanity-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Quitting the Rockefeller Foundation in February 1918, King became an independent consultant on labour issues for the next two years, earning $1,000 per week from leading American corporations. Even so, he kept his official residence in Ottawa, hoping for a call to duty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958255–265_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958255–265-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Wartime_politics">Wartime politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Wartime politics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1917, Canada was in crisis; King supported Liberal leader Wilfrid Laurier in his opposition to <a href="/wiki/Conscription" title="Conscription">conscription</a>, which was violently opposed in the province of <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>. The Liberal party became deeply split, with several <a href="/wiki/Anglophone" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglophone">Anglophones</a> joining the pro-conscription <a href="/wiki/Unionist_Party_(Canada)" title="Unionist Party (Canada)">Union</a> government, a coalition controlled by the Conservatives under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Robert_Borden" title="Robert Borden">Robert Borden</a>. King returned to Canada to run in the <a href="/wiki/1917_Canadian_federal_election" title="1917 Canadian federal election">1917 election</a>, which focused almost entirely on the conscription issue. Unable to overcome a landslide against Laurier, King lost in the constituency of <a href="/wiki/York_North" title="York North">York North</a>, which his grandfather had once represented.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Opposition_leader_.281919.E2.80.931921.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_leader_(1919–1921)">Opposition leader (1919–1921)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Opposition leader (1919–1921)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1919_leadership_election">1919 leadership election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: 1919 leadership election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Liberal Party was deeply divided by Quebec's opposition to conscription and the agrarian revolt in Ontario and the Prairies. Levin argues that when King returned to politics in 1919, he was a rusty outsider with a weak base facing a nation bitterly split by language, regionalism and class. He outmaneuvered more senior competitors by embracing Laurier's legacy, championing labour interests, calling for welfare reform, and offering solid opposition to the Conservative rivals.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> When Laurier died in 1919, King was elected leader in the first <a href="/wiki/1919_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1919 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">Liberal leadership convention</a>, defeating his three rivals on the fourth ballot. He won thanks to the support of the Quebec bloc, organized by <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Lapointe" title="Ernest Lapointe">Ernest Lapointe</a> (1876–1941), later King's long-time lieutenant in Quebec. King could not speak French, but in election after election for the next 20 years (save for 1930), Lapointe produced the critical seats to give the Liberals control of the Commons. When campaigning in Quebec, King portrayed Lapointe as co-prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-Betcherman_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Betcherman-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:King1919HeadShot.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/King1919HeadShot.jpg/150px-King1919HeadShot.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="208" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/King1919HeadShot.jpg/225px-King1919HeadShot.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/King1919HeadShot.jpg/300px-King1919HeadShot.jpg 2x" data-file-width="419" data-file-height="580" /></a><figcaption>King, 1919</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Idealizes_the_Prairies">Idealizes the Prairies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Idealizes the Prairies"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Once King became the Liberal leader in 1919 he paid closer attention to the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Prairies" title="Canadian Prairies">Prairies</a>, a fast-developing region. Viewing a sunrise in <a href="/wiki/Alberta" title="Alberta">Alberta</a> in 1920, he wrote in his diary, "I thought of the New Day, the New Social Order. It seems like Heaven's prophecy of the dawn of a new era, revealed to me."<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Pragmatism played a role as well, since his party depended for its survival on the votes of <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Party of Canada">Progressive Party</a> Members of Parliament, many of whom who represented farmers in Ontario and the Prairies. He convinced many Progressives to return to the Liberal fold.<sup id="cite_ref-Robert_A._Wardhaugh_2000_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Robert_A._Wardhaugh_2000-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1921_federal_election">1921 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: 1921 federal election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/1921_Canadian_federal_election" title="1921 Canadian federal election">1921 election</a>, King's Liberals defeated the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(historical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservative Party of Canada (historical)">Conservatives</a> led by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Meighen" title="Arthur Meighen">Arthur Meighen</a>, winning a narrow majority of 118 out of 235 seats. The Conservatives won 50, the newly formed Progressive Party won 58 (but declined to form the official Opposition), and the remaining ten seats went to Labour MPs and Independents; most of these ten supported the Progressives. King became prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281921.E2.80.931926.2C_1926.E2.80.931930.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1921–1926,_1926–1930)">Prime Minister (1921–1926, 1926–1930)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Prime Minister (1921–1926, 1926–1930)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>As prime minister of Canada, King was appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Council of the United Kingdom</a> on 20 June 1922<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> and was sworn at Buckingham Palace on October 11, 1923,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> during the <a href="/wiki/1923_Imperial_Conference" title="1923 Imperial Conference">1923 Imperial Conference</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Balancing_act">Balancing act</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Balancing act"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During his first term of office, from 1921 to 1926, King sought to lower wartime taxes and, especially, wartime ethnic and labour tensions. "The War is over", he argued, "and for a long time to come it is going to take all that the energies of man can do to bridge the chasm and heal the wounds which the War has made in our social life."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958p._294;_Letter_of_May_5,_1919_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958p._294;_Letter_of_May_5,_1919-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite prolonged negotiations, King was unable to attract the Progressives into his government, but once Parliament opened, he relied on their support to defeat <a href="/wiki/Non-confidence" class="mw-redirect" title="Non-confidence">non-confidence</a> motions from the Conservatives. King was opposed in some policies by the Progressives, who opposed the high <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a> of the <a href="/wiki/National_Policy" title="National Policy">National Policy</a>. King faced a delicate balancing act of reducing tariffs enough to please the Prairie-based Progressives, but not so much as to alienate his vital supporters in industrial Ontario and Quebec, who perceived tariffs were necessary to compete with American imports.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson195814,_15_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson195814,_15-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Hutchison_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutchison-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Over time, the Progressives gradually weakened. Their effective and passionate leader, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Crerar" title="Thomas Crerar">Thomas Crerar</a>, resigned to return to his grain business, and was replaced by the more placid <a href="/wiki/Robert_Forke" title="Robert Forke">Robert Forke</a>, who joined King's cabinet in 1926 as Minister of Immigration and Colonization after becoming a <a href="/wiki/Liberal-Progressive" title="Liberal-Progressive">Liberal-Progressive</a>. Socialist reformer <a href="/wiki/J._S._Woodsworth" title="J. S. Woodsworth">J.&#160;S. Woodsworth</a> gradually gained influence and power, and King was able to reach an accommodation with him on policy matters.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> In any event, the Progressive caucus lacked the party discipline that was traditionally enforced by the Liberals and Conservatives. The Progressives had campaigned on a promise that their MP's would represent their constituents first. King used this to his advantage, as he could always count on at least a handful of Progressive MP's to shore up his near-majority position for any crucial vote.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigration">Immigration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Immigration"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1923, King's government passed the <i><a href="/wiki/Chinese_Immigration_Act,_1923" title="Chinese Immigration Act, 1923">Chinese Immigration Act, 1923</a></i> banning most forms of <a href="/wiki/Chinese_people" title="Chinese people">Chinese</a> immigration to Canada. Immigration from most countries was controlled or restricted in some way, but only the Chinese were completely prohibited from immigrating. This was after various members of the federal and some provincial governments (especially <a href="/wiki/British_Columbia" title="British Columbia">British Columbia</a>) put pressure on the federal government to discourage Chinese immigration.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Also in 1923, the government modified the <i><a href="/wiki/History_of_Canadian_nationality_law" title="History of Canadian nationality law">Immigration Act</a></i> to allow former subjects of <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austria-Hungary</a> to once again enter Canada. Ukrainian immigration resumed after restrictions were put in place during World War I.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="City_planning">City planning</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: City planning"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>King had a long-standing concern with city planning and the development of the national capital, since he had been trained in the settlement house movement and envisioned town planning and garden cities as a component of his broader program of social reform. He drew on four broad traditions in early North American planning: social planning, the Parks Movement, the City Scientific, and the <a href="/wiki/City_Beautiful_movement" title="City Beautiful movement">City Beautiful</a>. King's greatest impact was as the political champion for the planning and development of Ottawa, Canada's national capital. His plans, much of which were completed in the two decades after his death, were part of a century of federal planning that repositioned Ottawa as a national space in the City Beautiful style. <a href="/wiki/Confederation_Square" title="Confederation Square">Confederation Square</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> for example, was initially planned to be a civic plaza to balance the nearby federal presence of Parliament Hill and was turned into a war memorial. The Great War monument was not installed until the 1939 royal visit, and King intended that the replanning of the capital would be the World War I memorial. However, the symbolic meaning of the World War I monument gradually expanded to become the place of remembrance for all Canadian war sacrifices and includes a war memorial.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Corruption_scandals">Corruption scandals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Corruption scandals"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>King called an <a href="/wiki/1925_Canadian_federal_election" title="1925 Canadian federal election">election in 1925</a>, in which the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(historical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservative Party of Canada (historical)">Conservatives</a> won the most seats, but not a majority in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons</a>. King held onto power with the support of the Progressives. A corruption scandal discovered late in his first term involved misdeeds around the expansion of the <a href="/wiki/Beauharnois_Canal" title="Beauharnois Canal">Beauharnois Canal</a> in Quebec; this led to extensive inquiries and eventually a <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Commission">Royal Commission</a>, which exposed the <a href="/wiki/Beauharnois_Scandal" class="mw-redirect" title="Beauharnois Scandal">Beauharnois Scandal</a>. The resulting press coverage damaged King's party in the election. Early in his second term, another <a href="/wiki/Corruption" title="Corruption">corruption</a> scandal, this time in the Department of Customs, was revealed, which led to more support for the Conservatives and Progressives, and the possibility that King would be forced to resign, if he lost sufficient support in the Commons. King had no personal connection to this scandal, although one of his own appointees was at the heart of it. Opposition leader Meighen unleashed his fierce invective towards King, stating he was hanging onto power "like a lobster with lockjaw".<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>`
</p>
<h3><span id="King.E2.80.93Byng_Affair"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="King–Byng_Affair">King–Byng Affair</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: King–Byng Affair"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/King%E2%80%93Byng_Affair" class="mw-redirect" title="King–Byng Affair">King–Byng Affair</a></div>
<p>In June 1926, King, facing a House of Commons vote connected to the customs scandal that could force his government to resign, advised the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a>, <a href="/wiki/Julian_H.G._Byng,_Viscount_Byng_of_Vimy" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian H.G. Byng, Viscount Byng of Vimy">Lord Byng</a>, to dissolve Parliament and call another election. Byng, however, declined the Prime Minister's request – the first time in <a href="/wiki/History_of_Canada" title="History of Canada">Canadian history</a> that a request for dissolution was refused; and, to date, the only time the governor general of Canada has done so. Byng instead asked <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">Leader of the Opposition</a>, Arthur Meighen, to form government. Although the Conservatives held more seats in the House than any other party, they did not control a majority. They were soon themselves defeated on a <a href="/wiki/Motion_of_non-confidence" class="mw-redirect" title="Motion of non-confidence">motion of non-confidence</a> on July 2. Meighen himself then requested a dissolution of Parliament, which Byng now granted.
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingCampaigning1926.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/KingCampaigning1926.jpg/250px-KingCampaigning1926.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="143" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/KingCampaigning1926.jpg/375px-KingCampaigning1926.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/KingCampaigning1926.jpg/500px-KingCampaigning1926.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="365" /></a><figcaption>King making a speech during his 1926 election campaign</figcaption></figure>
<p>King ran the <a href="/wiki/1926_Canadian_federal_election" title="1926 Canadian federal election">1926 Liberal election campaign</a> largely on the issue of the right of Canadians to govern themselves and against the interference of the Crown. The Liberal Party was returned to power with a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a>, which bolstered King's position on the issue and the position of the Prime Minister generally. King later pushed for greater Canadian autonomy at the <a href="/wiki/1926_Imperial_Conference" title="1926 Imperial Conference">1926 Imperial Conference</a> which elicited the <a href="/wiki/Balfour_Declaration_of_1926" title="Balfour Declaration of 1926">Balfour Declaration</a> stating that upon the granting of <a href="/wiki/Dominion" title="Dominion">dominion</a> status, Canada, Australia, New Zealand, <a href="/wiki/Newfoundland" class="mw-redirect" title="Newfoundland">Newfoundland</a>, South Africa, and the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Free_State" title="Irish Free State">Irish Free State</a>, while still autonomous communities within the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>, ceased to be subordinate to the United Kingdom. Thus, the governor general ceased to represent the British government and was solely the personal representative of the sovereign while becoming a representative of <a href="/wiki/The_Crown" title="The Crown">The Crown</a>. This ultimately was formalized in the <a href="/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_1931" title="Statute of Westminster 1931">Statute of Westminster 1931</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> On September 14, King and his party won the election with a plurality of seats in the Commons: 116 seats to the Conservatives' 91 in a 245-member House.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ImperialConference.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/ImperialConference.jpg/220px-ImperialConference.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/ImperialConference.jpg/330px-ImperialConference.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/ImperialConference.jpg/440px-ImperialConference.jpg 2x" data-file-width="635" data-file-height="506" /></a><figcaption>Mackenzie King (seated right) at the <a href="/wiki/1926_Imperial_Conference" title="1926 Imperial Conference">1926 Imperial Conference</a>, which led to the <a href="/wiki/Balfour_Declaration_of_1926" title="Balfour Declaration of 1926">Balfour Declaration</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Extending_Canadian_autonomy">Extending Canadian autonomy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Extending Canadian autonomy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingMasseyErsme_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/KingMasseyErsme_%28cropped%29.jpg/175px-KingMasseyErsme_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="175" height="211" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/KingMasseyErsme_%28cropped%29.jpg/263px-KingMasseyErsme_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/88/KingMasseyErsme_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="283" data-file-height="342" /></a><figcaption>British diplomat <a href="/wiki/Esme_Howard" class="mw-redirect" title="Esme Howard">Esme Howard</a>, King, and Canadian diplomat <a href="/wiki/Vincent_Massey" title="Vincent Massey">Vincent Massey</a>, first Canadian Envoy to the United States, at the Canadian Legation during a visit to Washington in 1927</figcaption></figure>
<p>During the <a href="/wiki/Chanak_Crisis" title="Chanak Crisis">Chanak Crisis</a> of 1922, King refused to support the British without first consulting <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada" title="Parliament of Canada">Parliament</a>, while the Conservative leader, Arthur Meighen, supported Britain. King sought a Canadian voice independent of <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a> in foreign affairs. In September 1922 the British Prime Minister, <a href="/wiki/David_Lloyd_George" title="David Lloyd George">David Lloyd George</a>, appealed repeatedly to King for Canadian support in the crisis. King coldly replied that the Canadian Parliament would decide what policy to follow, making clear it would not be bound by London's suggestions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958401–422_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958401–422-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> King wrote in his diary of the British appeal: "I confess it annoyed me. It is drafted designedly to play the imperial game, to test out centralization versus autonomy as regards European wars...No [Canadian] contingent will go without parliament being summoned in the first instance".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958409_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958409-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The British were disappointed with King's response but the crisis was soon resolved, as King had anticipated.<sup id="cite_ref-Hutchison_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutchison-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> After Chanak, King was concerned about the possibility that Canada might go to war because of its connections with Britain, writing to <a href="/wiki/Violet_Markham" title="Violet Markham">Violet Markham</a>:
</p>
<blockquote><p>Anything like centralization in London, to say nothing of a direct or indirect attempt on the part of those in office in Downing Street to tell the people of the Dominions what they should or should not do, and to dictate their duty in matters of foreign policy, is certain to prove just as injurious to the so-called 'imperial solidarity' as any attempt at interference in questions of purely domestic concern. If membership within the British Commonwealth means participation by the Dominions in any and every war in which Great Britain becomes involved, without consultation, conference, or agreement of any kind in advance, I can see no hope for an enduring relationship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDawson1958419_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawson1958419-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>For years, <a href="/wiki/Halibut" title="Halibut">halibut</a> stocks were depleting in Canadian and American fishing areas in the North <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Ocean" title="Pacific Ocean">Pacific Ocean</a>. In 1923, King's government negotiated the <a href="/wiki/Halibut_Treaty" title="Halibut Treaty">Halibut Treaty</a> with the United States. The treaty annually prohibited commercial fishing from November 16 to February 15; violation would result in seizure. The agreement was notable in which Canada negotiated it without a British delegate at the table and without ratification from the <a href="/wiki/British_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="British Parliament">British Parliament</a>; though not official, <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_convention_(political_custom)" title="Constitutional convention (political custom)">convention</a> stated that the United Kingdom would have a seat at the table or be a signatory to any agreement Canada was part of. King argued the situation only concerned Canada and the United States. After, the British accepted King's intentions to send a separate Canadian diplomat to <a href="/wiki/Washington_D.C." class="mw-redirect" title="Washington D.C.">Washington D.C.</a> (to represent Canada's interests) rather than a British one. At the <a href="/wiki/1923_Imperial_Conference" title="1923 Imperial Conference">1923 Imperial Conference</a>, Britain accepted the Halibut Treaty, arguing it set a new precedent for the role of <a href="/wiki/British_Dominion" class="mw-redirect" title="British Dominion">British Dominions</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGranatsteinHillmer199987,_95_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGranatsteinHillmer199987,_95-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King expanded the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_External_Affairs_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of External Affairs (Canada)">Department of External Affairs</a>, founded in 1909, to further promote Canadian autonomy from Britain. The new department took some time to develop, but over time it significantly increased the reach and projection of Canadian diplomacy.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Prior to this, Canada had relied on British diplomats who owed their first loyalty to London. After the King–Byng episode, King recruited many high-calibre people for the new venture, including future prime minister <a href="/wiki/Lester_Pearson" class="mw-redirect" title="Lester Pearson">Lester Pearson</a> and influential career administrators <a href="/wiki/Norman_Robertson" title="Norman Robertson">Norman Robertson</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hume_Wrong" class="mw-redirect" title="Hume Wrong">Hume Wrong</a>. This project was a key element of his overall strategy, setting Canada on a course independent of Britain, of former colonizer <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a>, as well as of the neighbouring powerful United States.<sup id="cite_ref-Hutchison_41-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutchison-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout his tenure, King led Canada from a dominion with responsible government to an autonomous nation within the <a href="/wiki/British_Commonwealth" class="mw-redirect" title="British Commonwealth">British Commonwealth</a>. King asserted Canadian autonomy against the British government's attempts to turn the Commonwealth into an alliance. His biographer asserts that "in this struggle Mackenzie King was the constant aggressor".<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> The Canadian High Commissioner to Britain, <a href="/wiki/Vincent_Massey" title="Vincent Massey">Vincent Massey</a>, claimed that an "anti-British bias" was "one of the most powerful factors in his make-up".<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_reforms">Other reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Other reforms"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingPensions1928.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/KingPensions1928.jpg/220px-KingPensions1928.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/KingPensions1928.jpg/330px-KingPensions1928.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/KingPensions1928.jpg/440px-KingPensions1928.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="484" /></a><figcaption>Signing of the Dominion–Provincial Agreement on <a href="/wiki/Old_age_security" class="mw-redirect" title="Old age security">old age pensions</a> in 1928. (Seated, L–R): <a href="/wiki/Peter_Heenan" title="Peter Heenan">Peter Heenan</a>, Thomas Donnelly, <a href="/wiki/John_Millar_(Canadian_politician)" title="John Millar (Canadian politician)">John Millar</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Richard_Motherwell" title="William Richard Motherwell">W. R. Motherwell</a>, William Lyon Mackenzie King, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Avery_Dunning" title="Charles Avery Dunning">C. A. Dunning</a>. (Standing, L–R): <a href="/wiki/John_Frederick_Johnston" title="John Frederick Johnston">Fred Johnson</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Vallance_(politician)" title="John Vallance (politician)">John Vallance</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edward_James_Young" title="Edward James Young">Ed Young</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cameron_Ross_McIntosh" title="Cameron Ross McIntosh">C. R. McIntosh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Robert_McKenzie_(Canadian_politician)" title="Robert McKenzie (Canadian politician)">Robert McKenzie</a>, <a href="/wiki/J._Gordon_Ross" title="J. Gordon Ross">Gordon Ross</a>, <a href="/wiki/Albert_Frederick_Totzke" title="Albert Frederick Totzke">A. F. Totzke</a>, <a href="/wiki/George_Washington_McPhee" title="George Washington McPhee">George McPhee</a>, <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_McLean_(politician)" title="Malcolm McLean (politician)">Malcolm McLean</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_George_Bock" title="William George Bock">William Bock</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In domestic affairs, King strengthened the Liberal policy of increasing the powers of the provincial governments by transferring to the governments of <a href="/wiki/Manitoba" title="Manitoba">Manitoba</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alberta" title="Alberta">Alberta</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Saskatchewan" title="Saskatchewan">Saskatchewan</a> the ownership of the crown lands within those provinces, as well as the subsoil rights; these in particular would become increasingly important, as petroleum and other natural resources proved very abundant. In collaboration with the provincial governments, he inaugurated a system of <a href="/wiki/Old_Age_Security" title="Old Age Security">old-age pensions</a> based on need.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> In February 1930, he appointed <a href="/wiki/Cairine_Wilson" title="Cairine Wilson">Cairine Wilson</a> as the first female <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">senator</a> in Canadian history.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:King60th.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/King60th.jpg/170px-King60th.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="241" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/King60th.jpg/255px-King60th.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/King60th.jpg/340px-King60th.jpg 2x" data-file-width="451" data-file-height="640" /></a><figcaption>King, in <a href="/wiki/Court_uniform_and_dress_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Court uniform and dress in the United Kingdom">court dress</a>, speaking on Parliament Hill during a ceremony celebrating the Diamond Jubilee of Confederation in 1927</figcaption></figure>
<p>Reductions in taxation were carried out such as exemptions under the sales tax on commodities and enlarged exemptions of income tax, while in 1929 taxes on cables, telegrams, and railway and steamship tickets were removed.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Measures were also carried out to support farmers. In 1922, for instance, a measure was introduced and passed "restoring the Crow's Nest Pass railways rates on grain and flour moving eastwards from the prairie provinces." A Farm Loan Board was set up to provide rural credit; advancing funds to farmers "at rates of interest and under terms not obtainable from the usual sources," while other measures were carried out such as preventative measures against foot and mouth disease and the establishment of grading standards "to assist in the marketing of agricultural products" both at home and overseas. In addition, the <i>Combines Investigation Act of 1923</i> was aimed at safeguarding consumers and producers from exploitation.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> A Fair Wages and Eight Hours Day Act was introduced in 1930.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Defeat_in_1930">Defeat in 1930</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Defeat in 1930"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>King's government was in power at the beginning of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression_in_Canada" title="Great Depression in Canada">Great Depression</a>, but was slow to respond to the mounting crisis. He felt that the crisis was a temporary swing of the business cycle and that the economy would soon recover without government intervention.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Critics said he was out of touch. Just prior to the election, King carelessly remarked that he "would not give a five-cent piece" to Tory provincial governments for unemployment relief.<sup id="cite_ref-Betcherman_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Betcherman-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The opposition made this remark a catch-phrase; the main issue was the deterioration in the economy and whether the prime minister was out of touch with the hardships of ordinary people.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> The Liberals lost the <a href="/wiki/1930_Canadian_federal_election" title="1930 Canadian federal election">election of 1930</a> to the Conservative Party, led by <a href="/wiki/Richard_Bedford_Bennett" class="mw-redirect" title="Richard Bedford Bennett">Richard Bedford Bennett</a>. The popular vote was very close between the two parties, with the Liberals actually earning more votes than in 1926, but the Conservatives had a geographical advantage that turned into enough seats to give a majority.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Opposition_leader_.281930.E2.80.931935.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_leader_(1930–1935)">Opposition leader (1930–1935)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Opposition leader (1930–1935)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>After his loss, King stayed on as leader of the Liberals, becoming the <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">leader of the Opposition</a> for the second time. King began his years as Opposition leader convinced that his government did not deserve defeat and that his government's financial caution helped the economy prosper. He blamed the financial crisis on the speculative excesses of businessmen and on the weather cycle. King argued that the worst mistake Canada could react to the Depression was to raise tariffs and restrict international trade. He believed that over time, voters would learn that they had been deceived by Bennett and would come to appreciate the King government's policy of frugality and <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BennettandWLMKing.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/BennettandWLMKing.jpg/225px-BennettandWLMKing.jpg" decoding="async" width="225" height="171" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/BennettandWLMKing.jpg/338px-BennettandWLMKing.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/BennettandWLMKing.jpg/450px-BennettandWLMKing.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="456" /></a><figcaption>Opposition leader King (right) and Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/R.B._Bennett" class="mw-redirect" title="R.B. Bennett">R.B. Bennett</a> (left), 1934</figcaption></figure>
<p>King's policy was to refrain from offering advice or alternative policies. Indeed, his policy preferences were not much different from Bennett's, and he let the Conservative government have its way. Though he gave the impression of sympathy with progressive and liberal causes, he had no enthusiasm for the <a href="/wiki/New_Deal" title="New Deal">New Deal</a> of American President <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a> (which Bennett eventually tried to emulate, after floundering without solutions for several years), and he never advocated massive government action to alleviate the Depression in Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Opposition leader, King denounced the Bennett government's <a href="/wiki/Deficit_spending" title="Deficit spending">budget deficits</a> as irresponsible, though didn't suggest his own idea of how budgets could be <a href="/wiki/Government_budget_balance" title="Government budget balance">balanced</a>. King also denounced the "blank cheques" parliament was asked to approve for relief and delayed the passage of these bills despite the objections of some Liberals who feared the public might conclude that the Liberals had no sympathy for those struggling. Each year, after the throne speech and the budget, King introduced amendments that blamed the depression on Bennett's policy of high tariffs.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By the time the <a href="/wiki/1935_Canadian_federal_election" title="1935 Canadian federal election">1935 election</a> arrived, the Bennett government was heavily unpopular due to their handling of the depression. Using the slogan "King or Chaos",<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> the Liberals won a <a href="/wiki/Landslide_victory" title="Landslide victory">landslide victory</a>, winning 173 out of the Commons' 245 seats and reducing the Conservatives to a <a href="/wiki/Rump_party" title="Rump party">rump</a> of 40; this was the largest <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a> at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281935.E2.80.931948.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1935–1948)">Prime Minister (1935–1948)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Prime Minister (1935–1948)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>For the first time in his political career, King led an undisputed Liberal majority government. Upon his return to office in October 1935, he seemed to demonstrate a commitment (like <a href="/wiki/Franklin_Roosevelt" class="mw-redirect" title="Franklin Roosevelt">Franklin Roosevelt</a>) to the underprivileged, speaking of a new era where "poverty and adversity, want and misery are the enemies which liberalism will seek to banish from the land".<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Once again, King appointed himself as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_(Canada)" title="Minister of Foreign Affairs (Canada)">secretary of state for external affairs</a>; he held that post until 1946.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_reforms">Economic reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Economic reforms"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Free_trade">Free trade</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Free trade"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Promising a much-desired trade treaty with the U.S., the King government passed the 1935 <a href="/wiki/Reciprocity_(Canadian_politics)" title="Reciprocity (Canadian politics)">Reciprocal</a> Trade Agreement. It marked the turning point in Canadian-American economic relations, reversing the disastrous trade war of 1930–31, lowering tariffs, and yielding a dramatic increase in trade. More subtly, it revealed to the prime minister and President Roosevelt that they could work together well.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
