<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_programs">Social programs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Social programs"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>King's government introduced the National Employment Commission in 1936. As for the unemployed, King was hostile to federal relief.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> However, the first compulsory national <a href="/wiki/Unemployment_benefits" title="Unemployment benefits">unemployment insurance</a> program was instituted in August 1940 under the King government after a constitutional amendment was agreed to by all of the Canadian provinces, to concede to the federal government legislative power over unemployment insurance. New Brunswick, Alberta and Quebec had held out against the federal government's desire to amend the constitution but ultimately acceded to its request, Alberta being the last to do so. The <i><a href="/wiki/Constitution_Act,_1867" title="Constitution Act, 1867">British North America Act</a></i> Section 91 was amended by adding in a heading designated Number 2A simply in the words "Unemployment Insurance".<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Over the next thirteen years, a wide range of reforms similar to those association with the New Deal were realized during Mackenzie King's last period in office as prime minister. In 1937, the age for blind persons to qualify for old-age pensions was reduced to 40 in 1937, and later to 21 in 1947.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> In 1939, compulsory contributions for pensions for low-income widows and orphans were introduced (although these only covered the regularly employed) while depressed farmers were subsidized from that same year onwards. In 1944, family allowances were introduced. King had various arguments in favour of family allowances, one of which, as noted by one study, was that family allowances "would mean better food, clothing and medical and dental care for children in low-income families."<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> From 1948 the federal government subsidized medical services in the provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Spending_management">Spending management</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Spending management"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The provincial governments faced declining revenues and higher welfare costs. They needed federal grants and loans to reduce their deficits. In a December 1935 conference with the premiers, King announced that the federal grants would be increased until the spring of 1936. At this stage, King's main goal was to have a federal system in which each level of government would pay for its programs out of its own tax sources.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King only reluctantly accepted a <a href="/wiki/Keynesian_economics" title="Keynesian economics">Keynesian</a> solution that involved federal <a href="/wiki/Deficit_spending" title="Deficit spending">deficit spending</a>, tax cuts, and subsidies to the housing market.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> King and his <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">finance minister</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Avery_Dunning" title="Charles Avery Dunning">Charles Avery Dunning</a>, had planned to <a href="/wiki/Balanced_budget" title="Balanced budget">balance the budget</a> for 1938. However, some colleagues, to King's surprise, opposed that idea and instead favoured job creation to stimulate the economy, citing British economist <a href="/wiki/John_Maynard_Keynes" title="John Maynard Keynes">John Maynard Keynes</a>'s theory that governments could increase employment by spending during times of low private investment. In a politically motivated move, King accepted their arguments and hence ran deficits in both 1938 and 1939.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Workers">Workers</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Workers"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The various provinces were assisted by the <i>Federal Unemployment and Agricultural Assistance Act of 1938</i> and the <i>Youth Training Act of 1939</i> to create training programs for young persons,<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> while an amendment to the <i><a href="/wiki/Criminal_Code_(Canada)" title="Criminal Code (Canada)">Criminal Code</a></i> in May 1939 provided against refusal to hire, or dismissal, "solely because of a person's membership in a lawful trade-union or association".<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i>Vocational Training Co-ordination Act</i> of 1942 provided an impetus to the provinces to set up facilities for postsecondary vocational training,<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> and in 1948 the <i>Industrial Relations and Disputes Investigation Act</i> was passed, which safeguarded the rights of workers to join unions while requiring employers to recognize unions chosen by their employees.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Housing">Housing</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Housing"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The Federal Home Improvement Plan of 1937 provided subsidized rates of interest on rehabilitation loans to 66,900 homes, while the <i><a href="/wiki/National_Housing_Act_(Canada)" title="National Housing Act (Canada)">National Housing Act</a></i> of 1938 made provision for the building of low-rent housing.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Another Housing Act was later passed in 1944 with the intention of providing federally guaranteed loans or mortgages to individuals who wished to repair or construct dwellings through their own initiative.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Agriculture">Agriculture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Agriculture"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>While King opposed Bennett's <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Wheat_Board" title="Canadian Wheat Board">Canadian Wheat Board</a> in 1935, he accepted its operation. However, by 1938, the board had sold its holdings and King proposed returning to the open market. This angered <a href="/wiki/Western_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="Western Canadian">Western Canadian</a> farmers, who favoured a board that would give them a guaranteed minimum price, with the federal government covering any losses. Facing a public campaign to keep the board, King and his <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Agriculture_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Agriculture (Canada)">minister of agriculture</a>, <a href="/wiki/James_Garfield_Gardiner" title="James Garfield Gardiner">James Garfield Gardiner</a>, reluctantly extended the board's life and offered a minimum price that would protect the farmers from further declines.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Crown_corporations">Crown corporations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Crown corporations"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1937, King's government established the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Canada_Air_Lines" title="Trans-Canada Air Lines">Trans-Canada Air Lines</a> (the precursor to <a href="/wiki/Air_Canada" title="Air Canada">Air Canada</a>), as a subsidiary of the <a href="/wiki/Crown_corporations_of_Canada" title="Crown corporations of Canada">crown corporation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Canadian_National_Railways" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian National Railways">Canadian National Railways</a>. It was created to provide air service to all regions of Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1938, King's government <a href="/wiki/Nationalize" class="mw-redirect" title="Nationalize">nationalized</a> the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_Canada" title="Bank of Canada">Bank of Canada</a> into a crown corporation.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Media_reforms">Media reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Media reforms"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1936, the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Radio_Broadcasting_Commission" title="Canadian Radio Broadcasting Commission">Canadian Radio Broadcasting Commission</a> (CRBC) became the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Canadian Broadcasting Corporation">Canadian Broadcasting Corporation</a> (CBC), which was a crown corporation. The CBC had a better organizational structure, more secure funding through the use of a licence fee on receiving sets (initially set at $2.50), and less vulnerability to political pressure.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> When Bennett's Conservatives were governing and the Liberals were in Opposition, the Liberals accused the network of being biased towards the Conservatives. During the 1935 election campaign, the CRBC broadcast a series of 15 minutes soap operas called <i>Mr. Sage</i> which were critical of King and the Liberal Party. Decried as political propaganda, the incident was one factor in King's decision to replace the CRBC.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1938, King's government invited British documentary maker <a href="/wiki/John_Grierson" title="John Grierson">John Grierson</a> to study the situation of the government's film production (which at that time was the responsibility of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Government_Motion_Picture_Bureau" title="Canadian Government Motion Picture Bureau">Canadian Government Motion Picture Bureau</a>). King believed that <a href="/wiki/Cinema_of_Canada" title="Cinema of Canada">Canadian cinema</a> deserved an increased presence in Canadian theatres.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> This report prompted the <i>National Film Act</i>, which created the <a href="/wiki/National_Film_Board_of_Canada" title="National Film Board of Canada">National Film Board of Canada</a> in 1939. It was created to produce and distribute films serving the national interest and was intended specifically to make Canada better known both domestically and internationally.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Gierson was appointed the first film commissioner in October 1939.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relationship_with_provinces">Relationship with provinces</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Relationship with provinces"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After 1936, the prime minister lost patience when <a href="/wiki/Western_Canada" title="Western Canada">Western Canadians</a> preferred radical alternatives such as the CCF (<a href="/wiki/Co-operative_Commonwealth_Federation" title="Co-operative Commonwealth Federation">Co-operative Commonwealth Federation</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Social_Credit" class="mw-redirect" title="Social Credit">Social Credit</a> to his middle-of-the-road liberalism. Indeed, he came close to writing off the region with his comment that the prairie dust bowl was "part of the U.S. desert area. I doubt if it will be of any real use again."<sup id="cite_ref-Robert_A._Wardhaugh_2000_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Robert_A._Wardhaugh_2000-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Instead he paid more attention to the industrial regions and the needs of Ontario and Quebec, particularly with respect to the proposed <a href="/wiki/St._Lawrence_Seaway" title="St. Lawrence Seaway">St. Lawrence Seaway</a> project with the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1937, <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Duplessis" title="Maurice Duplessis">Maurice Duplessis</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Conservatism_in_Canada" title="Conservatism in Canada">conservative</a> <a href="/wiki/Union_Nationale_(Quebec)" title="Union Nationale (Quebec)">Union Nationale</a> <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Quebec" title="Premier of Quebec">premier of Quebec</a>, passed the <a href="/wiki/Padlock_Law" title="Padlock Law">Padlock Law</a> (the <i>Act to Protect the Province Against Communistic Propaganda</i>),<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> which intimidated labour leaders by threatening to lock up their offices for any alleged communist activities. King's government, which had already repealed the section of the <i>Criminal Code</i> banning unlawful associations, considered disallowing this bill. However, King's cabinet minister, <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Lapointe" title="Ernest Lapointe">Ernest Lapointe</a>, believed this would harm the Liberal Party's electoral chances in Quebec. King and his English-Canadian ministers accepted Lapointe's view; as King wrote in his diary in July 1938, "we were prepared to accept what really should not, in the name of liberalism, be tolerated for one moment."<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Germany_and_Hitler">Germany and Hitler</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Germany and Hitler"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In March 1936, in response to the German <a href="/wiki/Remilitarization_of_the_Rhineland" class="mw-redirect" title="Remilitarization of the Rhineland">remilitarization of the Rhineland</a>, King had the <a href="/wiki/High_Commission_of_Canada_in_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="High Commission of Canada in the United Kingdom">High Commission of Canada in the United Kingdom</a> inform the British government that if Britain went to war with Germany over the <a href="/wiki/Rhineland" title="Rhineland">Rhineland</a> issue, Canada would remain neutral.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In June 1937, during an <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Conference" title="Imperial Conference">Imperial Conference</a> in London of the prime ministers of every dominion, King informed <a href="/wiki/British_Prime_Minister" class="mw-redirect" title="British Prime Minister">British Prime Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a> that Canada would only go to war if Britain were directly attacked, and that if Britain were to become involved in a continental war then Chamberlain was not to expect Canadian support.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mackenzie_King_in_Nazi_Germany.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Mackenzie_King_in_Nazi_Germany.jpg/200px-Mackenzie_King_in_Nazi_Germany.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="290" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Mackenzie_King_in_Nazi_Germany.jpg/300px-Mackenzie_King_in_Nazi_Germany.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/dd/Mackenzie_King_in_Nazi_Germany.jpg 2x" data-file-width="331" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>King (far left) at a ceremony in <a href="/wiki/Berlin" title="Berlin">Berlin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a>, 1937</figcaption></figure><p>
In 1937, King visited <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a> and met with <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-king_in_berlin_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-king_in_berlin-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>  Possessing a religious yearning for direct insight into the hidden mysteries of life and the universe, and strongly influenced by the operas of <a href="/wiki/Richard_Wagner" title="Richard Wagner">Richard Wagner</a> (who was also Hitler's favourite composer), King decided Hitler was akin to mythical <a href="/wiki/Wagnerian" class="mw-redirect" title="Wagnerian">Wagnerian</a> heroes within whom good and evil were struggling. He thought that good would eventually triumph and Hitler would redeem his people and lead them to a harmonious, uplifting future. These spiritual attitudes not only guided Canada's relations with Hitler but gave the prime minister the comforting sense of a higher mission, that of helping to lead Hitler to peace. King commented in his journal that "he is really one who truly loves his fellow-men, and his country, and would make any sacrifice for their good".<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> King forecast that: <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1211633275">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 32px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The world will yet come to see a very great man–mystic in Hitler&#160;... I cannot abide in Nazism&#160;– the regimentation&#160;– cruelty&#160;– oppression of Jews&#160;– attitude towards religion, etc., but Hitler&#160;... will rank some day with Joan of Arc among the deliverers of his people.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In late 1938, during the great crisis in Europe over <a href="/wiki/Czechoslovakia" title="Czechoslovakia">Czechoslovakia</a> that culminated in the <a href="/wiki/Munich_Agreement" title="Munich Agreement">Munich Agreement</a>, Canadians were divided. Francophones insisted on neutrality, as did some top advisers like <a href="/wiki/Oscar_D._Skelton" title="Oscar D. Skelton">Oscar D. Skelton</a>. Imperialists stood behind Britain and were willing to fight Germany. King, who served as his own secretary of state for external affairs (foreign minister), said privately that if he had to choose he would not be neutral, but he made no public statement. All of Canada was relieved that the British appeasement at Munich, while sacrificing the rights of Czechoslovakia, seemed to bring peace.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Under King's administration, the Canadian government, responding to strong public opinion, especially in Quebec, refused to expand immigration opportunities for <a href="/wiki/Jewish" class="mw-redirect" title="Jewish">Jewish</a> <a href="/wiki/Refugee" title="Refugee">refugees</a> from Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> In June 1939 Canada, along with <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a> and the United States, refused to allow entry for the 900 Jewish refugees aboard the passenger ship <a href="/wiki/MS_St._Louis" title="MS St. Louis">MS&#160;<i>St. Louis</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> King's government was widely criticized for its antisemitic policies and refusal to admit Jewish refugees. Most famously, when <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Blair" title="Frederick Blair">Frederick Blair</a>, an immigration official in King's party, was asked how many Jewish refugees Canada would admit after <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, he replied "None is too many". This policy was wholly supported by King and his political allies.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_World_War">Second World War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Second World War"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:GeorgeVIBanffSprings.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/GeorgeVIBanffSprings.jpg/220px-GeorgeVIBanffSprings.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/GeorgeVIBanffSprings.jpg/330px-GeorgeVIBanffSprings.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/70/GeorgeVIBanffSprings.jpg 2x" data-file-width="377" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>(From right to left) <a href="/wiki/George_VI" title="George VI">King George VI</a>, <a href="/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_The_Queen_Mother" title="Queen Elizabeth The Queen Mother">Queen Elizabeth</a>, and Prime Minister Mackenzie King in <a href="/wiki/Banff,_Alberta" title="Banff, Alberta">Banff, Alberta</a>, 1939</figcaption></figure><p> King accompanied the Royal Couple—King <a href="/wiki/George_VI" title="George VI">George VI</a> and Queen Elizabeth—throughout their 1939 cross-Canada tour, as well as on their American visit, a few months before the start of World War II.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Quebec_Conference,_Canada,_August_1943_TR1347.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/The_Quebec_Conference%2C_Canada%2C_August_1943_TR1347.jpg/220px-The_Quebec_Conference%2C_Canada%2C_August_1943_TR1347.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/The_Quebec_Conference%2C_Canada%2C_August_1943_TR1347.jpg/330px-The_Quebec_Conference%2C_Canada%2C_August_1943_TR1347.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/The_Quebec_Conference%2C_Canada%2C_August_1943_TR1347.jpg/440px-The_Quebec_Conference%2C_Canada%2C_August_1943_TR1347.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4683" data-file-height="3688" /></a><figcaption>King (back left) with (counterclockwise from King) <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a>, Governor General <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Cambridge,_1st_Earl_of_Athlone" title="Alexander Cambridge, 1st Earl of Athlone">the Earl of Athlone</a> and <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Conference,_1943" class="mw-redirect" title="Quebec Conference, 1943">Quebec Conference in 1943</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Octagon2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Octagon2.jpg/220px-Octagon2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="185" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Octagon2.jpg/330px-Octagon2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Octagon2.jpg/440px-Octagon2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="1077" /></a><figcaption>King (far right) together with (from left to right) Governor General <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Cambridge,_1st_Earl_of_Athlone" title="Alexander Cambridge, 1st Earl of Athlone">the Earl of Athlone</a>, <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Second_Quebec_Conference" title="Second Quebec Conference">Octagon Conference</a>, Quebec City, September 1944</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_and_Norman_Robertson_attending_the_Commonwealth_Prime_Ministers%E2%80%99_Conference_-_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_et_Norman_Robertson_assistant_%C3%A0_la_conf%C3%A9rence_des_premiers_ministres_du_Commonwealth_(25134542897).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_and_Norman_Robertson_attending_the_Commonwealth_Prime_Ministers%E2%80%99_Conference_-_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_et_Norman_Robertson_assistant_%C3%A0_la_conf%C3%A9rence_des_premiers_ministres_du_Commonwealth_%2825134542897%29.jpg/220px-thumbnail.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_and_Norman_Robertson_attending_the_Commonwealth_Prime_Ministers%E2%80%99_Conference_-_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_et_Norman_Robertson_assistant_%C3%A0_la_conf%C3%A9rence_des_premiers_ministres_du_Commonwealth_%2825134542897%29.jpg/330px-thumbnail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_and_Norman_Robertson_attending_the_Commonwealth_Prime_Ministers%E2%80%99_Conference_-_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_et_Norman_Robertson_assistant_%C3%A0_la_conf%C3%A9rence_des_premiers_ministres_du_Commonwealth_%2825134542897%29.jpg/440px-thumbnail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2556" data-file-height="1907" /></a><figcaption>Canadian diplomat <a href="/wiki/Norman_Robertson" title="Norman Robertson">Norman Robertson</a> and Mackenzie King, 1944</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CommonwealthPrimeMinisters1944.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/CommonwealthPrimeMinisters1944.jpg/220px-CommonwealthPrimeMinisters1944.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/CommonwealthPrimeMinisters1944.jpg/330px-CommonwealthPrimeMinisters1944.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/CommonwealthPrimeMinisters1944.jpg/440px-CommonwealthPrimeMinisters1944.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="470" /></a><figcaption>King, sitting left, at the <a href="/wiki/1944_Commonwealth_Prime_Ministers%27_Conference" title="1944 Commonwealth Prime Ministers&#39; Conference">1944 Commonwealth Prime Ministers' Conference</a></figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Declaration_of_war">Declaration of war</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Declaration of war"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>According to historian <a href="/wiki/Norman_Hillmer" title="Norman Hillmer">Norman Hillmer</a>, as British Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a> "negotiated in Munich with Adolf Hitler in September 1938, Mackenzie King, Canada's Prime Minister, grew agitated."<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> King realized the likelihood of <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a> and began mobilizing on August 25, 1939, with full mobilization on September 1, 1939, the day Germany invaded Poland. In 1914, Canada was at war by virtue of King <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">George V</a>'s declaration. In 1939, King asserted Canada's autonomy and convened the House of Commons on September 7, nearly a month ahead of schedule, to discuss the government's intention to enter the war. King affirmed Canadian autonomy by saying that the Canadian Parliament would make the final decision on the issue of going to war. He reassured the pro-British Canadians that Parliament would surely decide that Canada would be at Britain's side if Great Britain was drawn into a major war. At the same time, he reassured those who were suspicious of British influence in Canada by promising that Canada would not participate in British colonial wars. His <a href="/wiki/Quebec_lieutenant" title="Quebec lieutenant">Quebec lieutenant</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Lapointe" title="Ernest Lapointe">Ernest Lapointe</a>, promised French Canadians that the government would not introduce conscription for overseas service; individual participation would be voluntary. These promises made it possible for Parliament to agree almost unanimously to <a href="/wiki/Canadian_declaration_of_war_on_Germany" title="Canadian declaration of war on Germany">declare war</a> on September 9. On September 10, King, through his high commissioner in London, issued a request to King George VI, asking him, in his capacity as King of Canada, to <a href="/wiki/Canadian_declaration_of_war_on_Germany" title="Canadian declaration of war on Germany">declare Canada at war against Germany</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Foreign policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>To re-arm Canada, King built the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Air_Force" title="Royal Canadian Air Force">Royal Canadian Air Force</a> as a viable military power, while at the same time keeping it separate from Britain's <a href="/wiki/Royal_Air_Force" title="Royal Air Force">Royal Air Force</a>. He was instrumental in obtaining the <a href="/wiki/British_Commonwealth_Air_Training_Plan" title="British Commonwealth Air Training Plan">British Commonwealth Air Training Plan</a> Agreement, which was signed in Ottawa in December 1939, binding Canada, Britain, New Zealand and Australia to a program that eventually trained half the airmen from those four nations in the Second World War.<sup id="cite_ref-P._Stacey,_1970_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-P._Stacey,_1970-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King linked Canada more and more closely to the United States, signing <a href="/wiki/Ogdensburg_Agreement" title="Ogdensburg Agreement">an agreement</a> with Roosevelt at <a href="/wiki/Ogdensburg,_New_York" title="Ogdensburg, New York">Ogdensburg, New York</a>, in August 1940 that provided for the close cooperation of Canadian and American forces, despite the fact that the U.S. remained officially neutral until the bombing of <a href="/wiki/Pearl_Harbor" title="Pearl Harbor">Pearl Harbor</a> on December 7, 1941. During the war the Americans took virtual control of the <a href="/wiki/Yukon" title="Yukon">Yukon</a> in building the <a href="/wiki/Alaska_Highway" title="Alaska Highway">Alaska Highway</a>, and major airbases in <a href="/wiki/Dominion_of_Newfoundland" title="Dominion of Newfoundland">Newfoundland</a>, at that time under British governance.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King—and Canada—were largely ignored by <a href="/wiki/British_Prime_Minister" class="mw-redirect" title="British Prime Minister">British Prime Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a>, despite Canada's major role in supplying food,<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> raw materials, munitions,<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> and money<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> to the hard-pressed British economy, <a href="/wiki/British_Commonwealth_Air_Training_Plan#Canada" title="British Commonwealth Air Training Plan">training airmen for the Commonwealth</a>, guarding the western half of the <a href="/wiki/North_Atlantic_Ocean" class="mw-redirect" title="North Atlantic Ocean">North Atlantic Ocean</a> against German <a href="/wiki/U-boat" title="U-boat">U-boats</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> and providing combat troops for the invasions of Italy, France and Germany in 1943–45. King proved highly successful in mobilizing the economy for war, with impressive results in industrial and agricultural output. The depression ended, prosperity returned, and Canada's economy expanded significantly.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the war, Canada rapidly expanded its diplomatic missions abroad. While Canada hosted two major Allied conferences in Quebec in 1943 and 1944, neither King nor his senior generals and admirals were invited to take part in any of the discussions.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_affairs">Political affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Political affairs"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>King's government made an unprecedented intervention in the <a href="/wiki/1939_Quebec_general_election" title="1939 Quebec general election">1939 Quebec general election</a> to defeat anti-war Premier Maurice Duplessis's Union Nationale and ensure victory for the pro-war <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Liberal_Party" title="Quebec Liberal Party">Quebec Liberals</a> under <a href="/wiki/Ad%C3%A9lard_Godbout" title="Adélard Godbout">Adélard Godbout</a>. Three of King's Cabinet ministers from Quebec (Ernest Lapointe, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Cardin" title="Arthur Cardin">Arthur Cardin</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Charles_Gavan_Power" title="Charles Gavan Power">Charles Gavan Power</a>) threatened to resign if Duplessis won re-election, claiming that no one would be left to stand up for Quebec in the Cabinet if conscription become an issue again.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> In his diary, King called Duplessis "diabolic" and a "little Hitler", believing Duplessis's aim was to provoke such a crisis between <a href="/wiki/French_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="French Canada">French Canada</a> and <a href="/wiki/English_Canada" title="English Canada">English Canada</a> that Quebec would leave Confederation.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> King used the powers of censorship under the <i><a href="/wiki/War_Measures_Act" title="War Measures Act">War Measures Act</a></i> to keep Duplessis from speaking on the radio. The Quebec Liberals won a landslide victory.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King rejected any notion of a <a href="/wiki/National_unity_government" title="National unity government">government of national unity</a> like the Unionist Government during World War&#160;I.<sup id="cite_ref-P._Stacey,_1970_109-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-P._Stacey,_1970-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> When the <a href="/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Ontario" title="Legislative Assembly of Ontario">Legislative Assembly of Ontario</a> passed a resolution criticizing King's government for not fighting the war "in the vigorous manner the people of Canada desire to see", King dissolved <a href="/wiki/18th_Canadian_Parliament" title="18th Canadian Parliament">the federal parliament</a>, triggering <a href="/wiki/1940_Canadian_federal_election" title="1940 Canadian federal election">a federal election for March 26, 1940</a>. He held it despite the ongoing war, unlike Britain, which formed a government of national unity and did not hold a wartime election. King won a second consecutive landslide victory, winning 179 seats&#160;&#8211;&#32; 6 more than in 1935. This was the Liberals' most successful result as of 2023<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> (in terms of proportion of seats). The <a href="/wiki/Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Official Opposition (Canada)">Official Opposition</a> party, the Conservatives, won the same number of seats as R.&#160;B. Bennett did in the 1935 election. King's relationship with <a href="/wiki/Ontario_Liberal_Party" title="Ontario Liberal Party">Liberal</a> Ontario Premier <a href="/wiki/Mitchell_Hepburn" title="Mitchell Hepburn">Mitchell Hepburn</a> was damaged due to Hepburn spearheading the resolution criticizing the war effort.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King promoted engineer and businessman <a href="/wiki/C._D._Howe" title="C. D. Howe">C.&#160;D. Howe</a> to senior cabinet positions during the war. King also suffered two cabinet setbacks; his <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_National_Defence_(Canada)" title="Minister of National Defence (Canada)">defence minister</a>, <a href="/wiki/Norman_McLeod_Rogers" title="Norman McLeod Rogers">Norman McLeod Rogers</a>, died in 1940 and his Quebec lieutenant and <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_and_Attorney_General_of_Canada" title="Minister of Justice and Attorney General of Canada">minister of justice and attorney general</a>, Ernest Lapointe, died in 1941. King successfully sought out the reluctant <a href="/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent" title="Louis St. Laurent">Louis St. Laurent</a>, a leading Quebec lawyer, to enter the House of Commons and to take over Lapointe's role. St. Laurent became King's right-hand man.<sup id="cite_ref-Hutchison_41-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutchison-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Wartime_expenditure">Wartime expenditure</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Wartime expenditure"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>On June 24, 1940, King's government presented the first $1 billion budget in Canadian history. It included $700 million in war expenses compared to $126 million in the 1939–1940 fiscal year; however, due to the war, the overall economy was the strongest in Canadian history.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Internment_of_Japanese-Canadians">Internment of Japanese-Canadians</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=39" title="Edit section: Internment of Japanese-Canadians"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Following the <a href="/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor" title="Attack on Pearl Harbor">attack on Pearl Harbor</a> in December 1941, Japanese Canadians were categorized as enemy aliens under the <i><a href="/wiki/War_Measures_Act" title="War Measures Act">War Measures Act</a></i>, which began to remove their personal rights.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Starting on December 8, 1941, 1,200 Japanese-Canadian-owned fishing vessels were impounded as a "defence measure."<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> On January 14, 1942, the federal government passed an order calling for the removal of male Japanese nationals between 18 and 45 years of age from a designated protected area of 100 miles inland from the British Columbia coast, enacted a ban against Japanese-Canadian fishing during the war, banned shortwave radios and controlled the sale of gasoline and dynamite to Japanese Canadians.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Japanese nationals removed from the coast after the January 14 order were sent to road camps around <a href="/wiki/Jasper,_Alberta" title="Jasper, Alberta">Jasper, Alberta</a>.
Three weeks later, on February 19, 1942, US President Franklin D. Roosevelt signed <a href="/wiki/Executive_Order_9066" title="Executive Order 9066">Executive Order 9066</a>, which called for the removal of <a href="/wiki/Internment_of_Japanese_Americans" title="Internment of Japanese Americans">110,000 people of Japanese ancestry</a> from the American coastline. A historian of internment, Ann Sunahara, argues that "the American action sealed the fate of Japanese Canadians."<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
On February 24, the federal government passed order-in-council PC 1468 which allowed for the <a href="/wiki/Japanese-Canadian_internment" class="mw-redirect" title="Japanese-Canadian internment">removal of "all persons of Japanese origin"</a><sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> This order-in-council allowed the <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Justice (Canada)">Minister of Justice</a> the broad powers of removing people from any protected area in Canada, but was meant for Japanese Canadians on the Pacific coast in particular. On February 25, the federal government announced that Japanese Canadians were being moved for reasons of national security.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> In all, some 27,000 people were detained without charge or trial, and their property confiscated. Others were deported to Japan.<sup id="cite_ref-Kobayashi_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kobayashi-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
King and his Cabinet received conflicting intelligence reports about the potential threat from the Japanese. Major General <a href="/wiki/Kenneth_Stuart" title="Kenneth Stuart">Ken Stuart</a> told Ottawa, "I cannot see that the Japanese Canadians constitute the slightest menace to national security."<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> In contrast, BC's attorney general, <a href="/wiki/Gordon_Sylvester_Wismer" title="Gordon Sylvester Wismer">Gordon Sylvester Wismer</a> reported that, while he had "the greatest respect for" and "hesitated to disagree with" the RCMP, "every law enforcement agency in this province, including ... the military officials charged with local internal security, are unanimous that a grave menace exists."<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Expansion_of_scientific_research">Expansion of scientific research</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=40" title="Edit section: Expansion of scientific research"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>King's government greatly expanded the role of the <a href="/wiki/National_Research_Council_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="National Research Council of Canada">National Research Council of Canada</a> during the war, moving into full-scale research in nuclear physics and commercial use of <a href="/wiki/Nuclear_power" title="Nuclear power">nuclear power</a> in the following years. King, with <a href="/wiki/C._D._Howe" title="C. D. Howe">C. D. Howe</a> acting as point man, moved the nuclear group from <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a> to <a href="/wiki/Chalk_River,_Ontario" class="mw-redirect" title="Chalk River, Ontario">Chalk River, Ontario</a> in 1944, with the establishment of <a href="/wiki/Chalk_River_Nuclear_Laboratories" class="mw-redirect" title="Chalk River Nuclear Laboratories">Chalk River Nuclear Laboratories</a> and the residential town of <a href="/wiki/Deep_River,_Ontario" title="Deep River, Ontario">Deep River, Ontario</a>. Canada became a world leader in this field, with the <a href="/wiki/NRX" title="NRX">NRX</a> reactor becoming operational in 1947; at the time, NRX was the only operational nuclear reactor outside the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Conscription">Conscription</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=41" title="Edit section: Conscription"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1944" title="Conscription Crisis of 1944">Conscription Crisis of 1944</a></div>
<p>King's promise not to impose conscription contributed to the defeat of <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Duplessis" title="Maurice Duplessis">Maurice Duplessis</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Union_Nationale_(Quebec)" title="Union Nationale (Quebec)">Union Nationale</a> Quebec provincial government in 1939 and the Liberals' re-election in the 1940 election. But after the fall of France in 1940, Canada introduced conscription for home service (conscription meant for the defence of Canada only). Only volunteers were to be sent overseas. King wanted to avoid a repeat of the <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1917" title="Conscription Crisis of 1917">Conscription Crisis of 1917</a>. By 1942, the military was pressing King hard to send conscripts to Europe. In 1942, King held <a href="/wiki/1942_Canadian_conscription_plebiscite" title="1942 Canadian conscription plebiscite">a national plebiscite</a> on the issue, asking the nation to relieve him of the commitment he had made during the election campaign. In the House of Commons on June 10, 1942, he said that his policy was "not necessarily conscription but conscription if necessary".<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingVEDay.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/KingVEDay.jpg/190px-KingVEDay.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="229" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/KingVEDay.jpg/285px-KingVEDay.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/KingVEDay.jpg/380px-KingVEDay.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1245" data-file-height="1500" /></a><figcaption>King making his address to Canada on <a href="/wiki/VE-Day" class="mw-redirect" title="VE-Day">VE-Day</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>French Canadians voted against conscription, with over 70 percent opposed, but an overwhelming majority – over 80 percent – of English Canadians supported it. French and English conscripts were sent to fight in the <a href="/wiki/Aleutian_Islands" title="Aleutian Islands">Aleutian Islands</a> in 1943 – technically North American soil and therefore not "overseas" – but the mix of Canadian volunteers and draftees found that the Japanese troops had fled before their arrival. Otherwise, King continued with a campaign to recruit volunteers, hoping to address the problem with the shortage of troops caused by heavy losses in the <a href="/wiki/Dieppe_Raid" title="Dieppe Raid">Dieppe Raid</a> in 1942, in Italy in 1943, and after the <a href="/wiki/Invasion_of_Normandy" class="mw-redirect" title="Invasion of Normandy">Battle of Normandy</a> in 1944. In November 1944, the government decided it was necessary to send conscripts for the war. This led to a brief political crisis (see <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1944" title="Conscription Crisis of 1944">Conscription Crisis of 1944</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Terrace_Mutiny" class="mw-redirect" title="Terrace Mutiny">a mutiny</a> by conscripts posted in British Columbia, but the war ended a few months later. In all, 12,908 conscripts were sent to fight abroad, though only 2,463 saw combat.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-war_Canada">Post-war Canada</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=42" title="Edit section: Post-war Canada"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1945_election">1945 election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=43" title="Edit section: 1945 election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>With the war winding down, King called <a href="/wiki/1945_Canadian_federal_election" title="1945 Canadian federal election">a federal election for June 11, 1945</a>. The Liberals' election campaign was centered on a broad program of <a href="/wiki/Social_security" class="mw-redirect" title="Social security">social security</a>. Although King was hesitant for his government to expand its role in the economy and run <a href="/wiki/Deficit_spending" title="Deficit spending">deficits</a>, he accepted it as these measures aligned with his concern for people struggling financially. There were political motives too; the Liberals needed to compete with the rising <a href="/wiki/Socialist" class="mw-redirect" title="Socialist">socialist</a> <a href="/wiki/Co-operative_Commonwealth_Federation" title="Co-operative Commonwealth Federation">Co-operative Commonwealth Federation</a> (CCF) for votes.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> In addition, King promised to commit one division of volunteers to <a href="/wiki/Operation_Downfall" title="Operation Downfall">Operation Downfall</a>, the planned invasion of Japan scheduled for late 1945-early 1946, whereas <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservative</a> leader <a href="/wiki/John_Bracken" title="John Bracken">John Bracken</a> promised conscription. Bracken's promise was unpopular and it thus benefited the Liberals.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Liberals were knocked down from a massive <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a> to a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a>. However, they were able to govern with a working majority with the support of eight "Independent Liberal" MPs (most of whom did not run as official Liberals because of their opposition to conscription). The Liberals' decline in support was partly attributed to the introduction of conscription, which was unpopular in many parts of Canada. As King was defeated in his own riding of <a href="/wiki/Prince_Albert_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Prince Albert (federal electoral district)">Prince Albert</a>, fellow Liberal <a href="/wiki/William_MacDiarmid" title="William MacDiarmid">William MacDiarmid</a>, who was re-elected in the <a href="/wiki/Safe_seat" title="Safe seat">safe seat</a> of <a href="/wiki/Glengarry_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Glengarry (federal electoral district)">Glengarry</a>, resigned so that an August 6 by-election could be held, which was subsequently won by King.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Foreign_affairs.2C_Cold_War"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs,_Cold_War">Foreign affairs, Cold War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=44" title="Edit section: Foreign affairs, Cold War"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>King helped found the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> (UN) in 1945 and attended the opening meetings in San Francisco.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Though he conceded that <a href="/wiki/Great_power" title="Great power">major powers</a> such as the United States and the United Kingdom would dominate the organization, King argued that <a href="/wiki/Middle_power" title="Middle power">middle powers</a> such as Canada should be given an influence on the UN based on their contributions to the settlement of disputes.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King moved Canada into the deepening <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> in alliance with the U.S. and Britain. He dealt with the espionage revelations of Soviet cipher clerk <a href="/wiki/Igor_Gouzenko" title="Igor Gouzenko">Igor Gouzenko</a>, who defected in Ottawa in September 1945, by quickly appointing a <a href="/wiki/Kellock-Taschereau_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Kellock-Taschereau Commission">Royal Commission</a> to investigate Gouzenko's allegations of a <a href="/wiki/Canadians" title="Canadians">Canadian</a> Communist spy-ring transmitting top-secret documents to Moscow. <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_and_Attorney_General_of_Canada" title="Minister of Justice and Attorney General of Canada">Justice Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent" title="Louis St. Laurent">Louis St. Laurent</a> dealt decisively with this crisis, the first of its type in Canada's history.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> St. Laurent succeeded King as external affairs minister in September 1946.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_achievements">Domestic achievements</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=45" title="Edit section: Domestic achievements"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>After the war, King quickly dismantled wartime controls. Unlike World War I, press censorship ended with the hostilities.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Serment_de_citoyennet%C3%A9_Citizenship_Oath_Mackenzie_King.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Serment_de_citoyennet%C3%A9_Citizenship_Oath_Mackenzie_King.jpg/220px-Serment_de_citoyennet%C3%A9_Citizenship_Oath_Mackenzie_King.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Serment_de_citoyennet%C3%A9_Citizenship_Oath_Mackenzie_King.jpg/330px-Serment_de_citoyennet%C3%A9_Citizenship_Oath_Mackenzie_King.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Serment_de_citoyennet%C3%A9_Citizenship_Oath_Mackenzie_King.jpg/440px-Serment_de_citoyennet%C3%A9_Citizenship_Oath_Mackenzie_King.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1656" data-file-height="1060" /></a><figcaption>King (far-left) becomes the first person to take the <a href="/wiki/Oath_of_Citizenship_(Canada)" title="Oath of Citizenship (Canada)">Oath of Citizenship</a>, from <a href="/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_Canada" title="Chief Justice of Canada">Chief Justice</a> <a href="/wiki/Thibaudeau_Rinfret" title="Thibaudeau Rinfret">Thibaudeau Rinfret</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court</a>, January 3, 1947</figcaption></figure>
<p>King's government introduced the <i><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Citizenship_Act_1946" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Citizenship Act 1946">Canadian Citizenship Act</a></i> in 1946, which officially created the notion of "<a href="/wiki/Canadian_nationality_law" title="Canadian nationality law">Canadian citizens</a>". Prior to this, Canadians were considered British subjects living in Canada. On January 3, 1947, King received Canadian citizenship certificate number 0001.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King also laid the groundwork for the <a href="/wiki/Dominion_of_Newfoundland" title="Dominion of Newfoundland">Dominion of Newfoundland</a>'s later entry into <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Confederation" title="Canadian Confederation">Canadian Confederation</a>, stating, "Newfoundlanders are no strangers to Canada, nor are Canadians strangers to Newfoundland." Pro-Confederation Newfoundlanders <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Gordon_Bradley" title="Frederick Gordon Bradley">Frederick Gordon Bradley</a> and <a href="/wiki/Joey_Smallwood" title="Joey Smallwood">Joey Smallwood</a> argued that joining Canada would raise the <a href="/wiki/Standard_of_living" title="Standard of living">standard of living</a> for Newfoundlanders; Britain also favoured Confederation. <a href="/wiki/1948_Newfoundland_referendums#The_second_referendum" title="1948 Newfoundland referendums">A runoff vote</a> was held on July 22, 1948, and 52.3 percent of voters decided that Newfoundland should enter Canada. After, Smallwood negotiated the terms of entry with King. Newfoundland entered Confederation on March 31, 1949, becoming Canada's tenth province.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=46" title="Edit section: Retirement"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>With his health declining, King declared in May 1948 that he would not be Liberal leader going in the next election.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/1948_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1948 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">The August 7, 1948, convention</a> (held exactly 29 years after King became Liberal leader) picked St. Laurent, King's personal choice, as the new leader of the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Three months later, on November 15, King retired after <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1154941027"><span class="frac">21<span class="sr-only">+</span><span class="num">1</span>&#8260;<span class="den">2</span></span> years as prime minister. King was the longest-serving prime minister in Canadian history; he also served in the most parliaments (six, in three non-consecutive periods) as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Retirement_and_death_.281948.E2.80.931950.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement_and_death_(1948–1950)">Retirement and death (1948–1950)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=47" title="Edit section: Retirement and death (1948–1950)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Grave_of_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Grave_of_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King.jpg/220px-Grave_of_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Grave_of_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King.jpg/330px-Grave_of_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Grave_of_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King.jpg/440px-Grave_of_William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2816" data-file-height="2112" /></a><figcaption>King's grave and memorial plaque</figcaption></figure>
<p>King had plans to write his memoirs. However, he did not enjoy a lengthy retirement and died on July 22, 1950, at <a href="/wiki/The_Farm_(Canada)" title="The Farm (Canada)">his country estate in Kingsmere</a><sup id="cite_ref-Neatby_3-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Neatby-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> from pneumonia.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> He is buried in <a href="/wiki/Mount_Pleasant_Cemetery,_Toronto" title="Mount Pleasant Cemetery, Toronto">Mount Pleasant Cemetery, Toronto</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Parks_Grave_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Parks_Grave-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_style_and_character">Personal style and character</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=48" title="Edit section: Personal style and character"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>King lacked a commanding presence or oratorical skills; he did not shine on the radio or in newsreels. There was scant charisma.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Cold and tactless in human relations, he had allies but very few close personal friends. His allies were annoyed by his constant intrigues.<sup id="cite_ref-English_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-English-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Scholars attribute King's long tenure as party leader to his wide range of skills that were appropriate to Canada's needs.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
King kept a very candid diary from 1893, when he was still an undergraduate, until a few days before his death in 1950; the volumes, stacked in a row, span a length of over seven metres and comprise over 50,000 manuscript pages of typed transcribed text.<sup id="cite_ref-LAC_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LAC-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> One biographer called these diaries "the most important single political document in twentieth-century Canadian history,"<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> for they explain motivations of the <a href="/wiki/Military_history_of_Canada_during_the_Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Military history of Canada during the Second World War">Canadian war efforts</a> and describe other events in detail.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingDandurand.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/KingDandurand.jpg/220px-KingDandurand.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/KingDandurand.jpg/330px-KingDandurand.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/KingDandurand.jpg/440px-KingDandurand.jpg 2x" data-file-width="880" data-file-height="1118" /></a><figcaption>King and Senator <a href="/wiki/Raoul_Dandurand" title="Raoul Dandurand">Raoul Dandurand</a> in state clothing, 1939.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_with_two_dogs_-_William_Mackenzie_King_avec_deux_chiens_(39107229965).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_with_two_dogs_-_William_Mackenzie_King_avec_deux_chiens_%2839107229965%29.jpg/175px-William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_with_two_dogs_-_William_Mackenzie_King_avec_deux_chiens_%2839107229965%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="175" height="176" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_with_two_dogs_-_William_Mackenzie_King_avec_deux_chiens_%2839107229965%29.jpg/263px-William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_with_two_dogs_-_William_Mackenzie_King_avec_deux_chiens_%2839107229965%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_with_two_dogs_-_William_Mackenzie_King_avec_deux_chiens_%2839107229965%29.jpg/350px-William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King_with_two_dogs_-_William_Mackenzie_King_avec_deux_chiens_%2839107229965%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2137" data-file-height="2150" /></a><figcaption>King with his two dogs, 1938</figcaption></figure>
<p>King's <a href="/wiki/Occult" title="Occult">occult</a> interests were kept secret during his years in office,<sup id="cite_ref-mallinos_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mallinos-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> and only became publicized after his death when his diaries were opened. Readers were amazed and for some, King was saddled with the moniker "Weird Willie."<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> King communed with spirits, using seances with paid mediums. Thereby, he claimed to have communicated with <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>, his dead mother, his grandfather, and several of his dead dogs, as well as the spirit of the late <a href="/wiki/Franklin_Delano_Roosevelt" class="mw-redirect" title="Franklin Delano Roosevelt">President Roosevelt</a>. Some historians argue that he sought personal reassurance from the spirit world, more than political advice. After his death, one of his mediums said that she had not realized that he was a politician. King did inquire whether his party would win the 1935 election, one of the few times politics came up during his seances. However Allan Levine argues that sometimes he did pay attention to the political implications of his seances: "All of his spiritualist experiences, his other superstitions and his multi-paranoid reactions imprinted on his consciousness, shaping his thoughts and feelings in a thousand different ways."<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Historians have seen in his <a href="/wiki/Spiritualism_(movement)" title="Spiritualism (movement)">spiritualism</a> and occult activities a penchant for forging unities from antitheses, thus having latent political import. Historian <a href="/wiki/Charles_Perry_Stacey" title="Charles Perry Stacey">C.P. Stacey</a>, in his 1976 book <i>A Very Double Life</i> examined King's secret life in detail, argued that King did not allow his beliefs to influence his decisions on political matters. Stacey wrote that King entirely gave up his interests in the occult and spiritualism during World War II.<sup id="cite_ref-Stacey_1976_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stacey_1976-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King never married,<sup id="cite_ref-mallinos_151-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mallinos-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> but had several close female friends, including Joan Patteson, a married woman with whom he spent some of his leisure time; sometimes she served as hostess at his dinner parties.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> He did not have a wife who could be the hostess all the time and handle the many social obligations that he tried to downplay. Editor Charles Bowman reports that, "He felt the lack of a wife, particularly when social duties called for a hostess."<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some historians have interpreted passages in his diaries as suggesting that King regularly had sexual relations with prostitutes.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Others, also basing their claims on passages of his diaries, have suggested that King was in love with <a href="/wiki/Lord_Tweedsmuir" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Tweedsmuir">Lord Tweedsmuir</a>, whom he had chosen for appointment as <a href="/wiki/Governor_General" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor General">Governor General</a> in 1935.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=49" title="Edit section: Legacy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Historian <a href="/wiki/George_Stanley" title="George Stanley">George Stanley</a> argues that King's wartime policies, "may not have been exciting or satisfying, but they were effective and successful. That is why, practically alone among wartime governments, his continued to enjoy public support after as well as during the Second World War."<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Historian <a href="/wiki/Jack_Granatstein" class="mw-redirect" title="Jack Granatstein">Jack Granatstein</a> evaluates the King government's economic performance. He reports, "Canada's economic management was generally judged the most successful of all the countries engaged in the war."<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Historian <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Moore_(Canadian_historian)" title="Christopher Moore (Canadian historian)">Christopher Moore</a> says, "King had made 'Parliament will decide' his maxim, and he trotted it out whenever he wished to avoid a decision."<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> King was keenly sensitive to the nuances of public policy; he was a workaholic with a shrewd and penetrating intelligence and a profound understanding of the complexities of Canadian society.<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> His strength was apparent when he synthesized, built support for, and passed measures that had reached a level of broad national support. Advances in the welfare state were an example. His successors, especially Diefenbaker, Pearson, and Trudeau built the welfare state which he had advanced during the Second World War into the modern cradle-to-grave system.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Historian <a href="/wiki/H._Blair_Neatby" title="H. Blair Neatby">H. Blair Neatby</a> wrote, "Mackenzie King has continued to intrigue Canadians. Critics argue that his political longevity was achieved by evasions and indecision, and that he failed to provide creative leadership. His defenders argue that he gradually changed Canada, a difficult country to govern, while keeping the nation united."<sup id="cite_ref-thecanadianencyclopedia.com_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thecanadianencyclopedia.com-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King was ranked as the greatest Canadian Prime Minister by a survey of Canadian historians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGranatsteinHillmer1999_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGranatsteinHillmer1999-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> King was named a <a href="/wiki/Persons_of_National_Historic_Significance" title="Persons of National Historic Significance">Person of National Historic Significance</a> in 1968.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Memorials">Memorials</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=50" title="Edit section: Memorials"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-More_citations_needed_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="Special:EditPage/William Lyon Mackenzie King">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>&#32;in this section. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">December 2017</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>King's likeness is used on the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_fifty-dollar_note" title="Canadian fifty-dollar note">Canadian fifty-dollar note</a> since 1975.
</p><p>King left no published political memoirs, although his private diaries were extensively detailed. His main published work remains his 1918 book <i>Industry and Humanity</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Industry_and_Humanity_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Industry_and_Humanity-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the publication of King's diaries in the 1970s, several fictional works about him were published by Canadian writers. These included Elizabeth Gourlay's novel <i>Isabel</i>, <a href="/wiki/Allan_Stratton" title="Allan Stratton">Allan Stratton</a>'s play <i>Rexy</i> and Heather Robertson's trilogy <i>Willie: A Romance</i> (1983), <i>Lily: A Rhapsody in Red</i> (1986), and <i>Igor: A Novel of Intrigue</i> (1989).<sup id="cite_ref-Diary_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Diary-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1998, there was controversy over King's exclusion from a memorial to the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Conference,_1943" class="mw-redirect" title="Quebec Conference, 1943">Quebec Conference</a>, which was attended by King, Roosevelt, and Churchill. The monument was commissioned by the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_separatist" class="mw-redirect" title="Quebec separatist">sovereigntist</a> <a href="/wiki/Parti_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois" title="Parti Québécois">Parti Québécois</a> government of Quebec, which justified the decision on their interpretation that King was acting merely as a host for the meeting between Roosevelt and Churchill. Canadian federalists, however, accused the government of Quebec of trying to advance their own political agenda.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/OC_Transpo" title="OC Transpo">OC Transpo</a> has a <a href="/wiki/Ottawa_Rapid_Transit" class="mw-redirect" title="Ottawa Rapid Transit">Transitway</a> station named Mackenzie King due to its location on the <a href="/wiki/Mackenzie_King_Bridge" title="Mackenzie King Bridge">Mackenzie King Bridge</a>. It is adjacent to the <a href="/wiki/Rideau_Centre" title="Rideau Centre">Rideau Centre</a> in downtown Ottawa, Ontario.
