</p><p>The bridge across the <a href="/wiki/Rideau_Canal" title="Rideau Canal">Rideau Canal</a> in downtown Ottawa, built following World War II, is named in his honour to recognize his contributions to the land planning of the city of Ottawa.<sup id="cite_ref-Stacey_1976_154-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stacey_1976-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King bequeathed his private country retreat in <a href="/wiki/Kingsmere,_Quebec" title="Kingsmere, Quebec">Kingsmere, Quebec</a>, near Ottawa, to the Government of Canada and most of the estate was incorporated into the federally managed <a href="/wiki/Gatineau_Park" title="Gatineau Park">Gatineau Park</a>. King's summer home at Kingsmere, called "The Farm", now serves as the <a href="/wiki/Official_residence" title="Official residence">official residence</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_House_of_Commons_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Speaker of the House of Commons of Canada">Speaker of the House of Commons of Canada</a>. The Farm and its grounds are located within Gatineau Park but are not open to the public.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Home_of_Mackenzie_King_in_Gatineau.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Home_of_Mackenzie_King_in_Gatineau.jpg/220px-Home_of_Mackenzie_King_in_Gatineau.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Home_of_Mackenzie_King_in_Gatineau.jpg/330px-Home_of_Mackenzie_King_in_Gatineau.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Home_of_Mackenzie_King_in_Gatineau.jpg/440px-Home_of_Mackenzie_King_in_Gatineau.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a><figcaption>Home of William Lyon Mackenzie King in <a href="/wiki/Kingsmere,_Quebec" title="Kingsmere, Quebec">Kingsmere, Quebec</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Woodside_National_Historic_Site" title="Woodside National Historic Site">Woodside National Historic Site</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kitchener,_Ontario" title="Kitchener, Ontario">Kitchener, Ontario</a> was King's boyhood home. The estate has over 4.65 hectares of garden and parkland for exploring and relaxing, and the house has been restored to reflect life during King's era. There is a MacKenzie King Public School in the Heritage Park neighbourhood in Kitchener. Kitchener was known as Berlin until 1916.
</p><p>King was mentioned in the book <i><a href="/wiki/Alligator_Pie" title="Alligator Pie">Alligator Pie</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Dennis_Lee_(author)" title="Dennis Lee (author)">Dennis Lee</a>, appearing as the subject of a <a href="/wiki/Literary_nonsense" title="Literary nonsense">nonsensical</a> children's poem,<sup id="cite_ref-Diary_166-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Diary-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> which reads "William Lyon Mackenzie King / He sat in the middle and played with string / He loved his mother like anything / William Lyon Mackenzie King."
</p><p>King is a prominent character in <a href="/wiki/Donald_Jack" title="Donald Jack">Donald Jack</a>'s novel <i>Me Too</i>, set in Ottawa in the 1920s.
</p><p>A character who appeared twice in the popular 1990s Canadian television series <i><a href="/wiki/Due_South" title="Due South">Due South</a></i> was named "Mackenzie King" in obvious reference.
</p><p>King is portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Sean_McCann_(actor)" title="Sean McCann (actor)">Sean McCann</a> in <a href="/wiki/Donald_Brittain" title="Donald Brittain">Donald Brittain</a>'s 1988 television miniseries <i><a href="/wiki/The_King_Chronicle" title="The King Chronicle">The King Chronicle</a></i>,<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> and by <a href="/wiki/Dan_Beirne" title="Dan Beirne">Dan Beirne</a> in <a href="/wiki/Matthew_Rankin" title="Matthew Rankin">Matthew Rankin</a>'s 2019 film <i><a href="/wiki/The_Twentieth_Century_(film)" title="The Twentieth Century (film)">The Twentieth Century</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Supreme_Court_appointments">Supreme Court appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King&amp;action=edit&amp;section=53" title="Edit section: Supreme Court appointments"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>King chose the following jurists to be appointed as justices of the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a>:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Arthur_Cyrille_Albert_Malouin" class="mw-redirect" title="Arthur Cyrille Albert Malouin">Arthur Cyrille Albert Malouin</a> (January 30, 1924 – October 1, 1924)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Francis_Alexander_Anglin" title="Francis Alexander Anglin">Francis Alexander Anglin</a> (as Chief Justice, September 16, 1924 – February 28, 1933; appointed a <a href="/wiki/Puisne_Justice" class="mw-redirect" title="Puisne Justice">Puisne Justice</a> under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Laurier</a>, February 23, 1909)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Edmund_Leslie_Newcombe" title="Edmund Leslie Newcombe">Edmund Leslie Newcombe</a> (September 16, 1924 – December 9, 1931)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Thibaudeau_Rinfret" title="Thibaudeau Rinfret">Thibaudeau Rinfret</a> (October 1, 1924 – June 22, 1954; appointed as Chief Justice January 8, 1944)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_Henderson_Lamont" title="John Henderson Lamont">John Henderson Lamont</a> (April 2, 1927 – March 10, 1936)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Robert_Smith_(Canadian_politician,_Stormont)" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert Smith (Canadian politician, Stormont)">Robert Smith</a> (May 18, 1927 – December 7, 1933)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lawrence_Arthur_Dumoulin_Cannon" title="Lawrence Arthur Dumoulin Cannon">Lawrence Arthur Dumoulin Cannon</a> (January 14, 1930 – December 25, 1939)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Albert_Blellock_Hudson" class="mw-redirect" title="Albert Blellock Hudson">Albert Blellock Hudson</a> (March 24, 1936 – January 6, 1947)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Robert_Taschereau" title="Robert Taschereau">Robert Taschereau</a> (February 9, 1940 – September 1, 1967)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ivan_Rand" title="Ivan Rand">Ivan Rand</a> (April 22, 1943 – April 27, 1959)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Roy_Lindsay_Kellock" class="mw-redirect" title="Roy Lindsay Kellock">Roy Lindsay Kellock</a> (October 3, 1944 – January 15, 1958)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/James_Wilfred_Estey" title="James Wilfred Estey">James Wilfred Estey</a> (October 6, 1944 – January 22, 1956)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Holland_Locke" title="Charles Holland Locke">Charles Holland Locke</a> (June 3, 1947 – September 16, 1962)</li></ul>

<ul><li><i>The Canadian Annual Review of Public Affairs</i> (annual, 1901–1938), <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=OzMhdbbnG2wC&amp;q=intitle:The+intitle:Canadian+intitle:Annual+intitle:Review">full text for 1920 online and downloadable</a>.</li>
<li>Mackenzie King, W. L. <i>Industry and Humanity: A Study in the Principles Under-Lying Industrial Reconstruction</i> (1918) <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.questia.com/read/3886991?title=Industry%20and%20Humanity%3a%20A%20Study%20in%20the%20Principles%20Under-Lying%20Industrial%20Reconstruction">online edition</a> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20110605090428/http://www.questia.com/read/3886991?title=Industry%20and%20Humanity:%20A%20Study%20in%20the%20Principles%20Under-Lying%20Industrial%20Reconstruction">Archived</a> June 5, 2011, at the <a href="/wiki/Wayback_Machine" title="Wayback Machine">Wayback Machine</a>; also <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archive.org/details/industryandhuma00kinggoog">full text online and downloadable</a>.</li>
<li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.collectionscanada.gc.ca/databases/king/index-e.html/"><i>The diaries of William Lyon Mackenzie King</i></a>, 50,000 pages, typescript; fully searchable.</li>
<li>Pickersgill, J.W., and Donald F. Forster, <i>The Mackenzie King Record</i>. 4 vols. Vol. 1: 1939–1944 and Vol. 2: 1944–1945 (University of Toronto Press, 1960); and <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20071209003442/http://www.questia.com/PM.qst?a=o&amp;d=1008334">Vol. 3: 1945–1946 online</a> and <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20100408092632/http://www.questia.com/PM.qst?a=o&amp;d=65373881">Vol. 4: 1946–1947 online</a> (University of Toronto Press, 1970). Edited from King's private diary.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Department_of_External_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Department of External Affairs">Canadian Department of External Affairs</a>, <i>Documents on Canadian External Relations</i> (Ottawa: Queen's Printer, 1967–). These cover the period 1909–1960. (Often referred to as <i>DCER</i>.)</li>
<li>Henderson, George F. ed <i>W.L. Mackenzie King: a bibliography and research guide</i> (2nd ed. University of Toronto Press, 2015); 392pp <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=6uqMBgAAQBAJ">excerpt and text search</a></li>
<li>Hou, Charles, and Cynthia Hou, eds. <i>Great Canadian Political Cartoons, 1915 to 1945.</i> (2002). 244pp.</li>
<li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://central.bac-lac.gc.ca/.redirect?app=fonandcol&amp;id=98362&amp;lang=eng">William Lyon Mackenzie King fonds</a> at <a href="/wiki/Library_and_Archives_Canada" title="Library and Archives Canada">Library and Archives Canada</a>.</li></ul>

</div></td></tr><tr><td class="navbox-abovebelow" colspan="2" style="font-size:smaller;"><div><sup>1</sup>Until 1909, the office of the minister of Labour was a secondary function of the Postmaster-General of Canada. W. L. M. King was the first to hold the office independently. <br />
<p><sup>2</sup>The office of Minister of Employment and Immigration, and Minister of Labour were abolished and the office of Minister of Human Resources Development went in force on July 12, 1996. Under the new provisions, a Minister of Labour may be appointed. However, when no Minister of Labour is appointed, the Minister of Human Resources Development shall exercise the powers and perform the duties and functions of the Minister of Labour.<br />
</p>
<sup>3</sup>Styled "Minister of Labour and Housing".</div></td></tr></tbody></table></div>
